- Ilham Khuri-Makdisi: The Nahda, the Press, and the Construction of a Radical Worldview
6. Q: What major institutional transition occurred in Jerusalem during the nineteenth century?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a shift from a long-standing system in which Islamic courts, religious education and traditional intellectual pursuits held substantial authority toward a more centralized order that incorporated Western-influenced courts, government schools, modern bureaucracy and new forms of scholarship. The transition was neither immediate nor uniform. Older institutions continued to operate, and local notables adapted to the new arrangements. The change affected careers, social prestige and access to power: people trained in modern state administration or foreign-language education increasingly gained influence alongside those whose standing had rested on religious learning. The chapter studies this as a reorganization of institutions and authority, not simply as cultural imitation of Europe.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the institutions that lost some of their former authority and the new institutions that gained responsibilities during the period."
Key point:
- The nineteenth-century transition changed the organization of law, education and government.
- Older and newer institutions overlapped for an extended period.
Source links:
- Koçak and Sancaktutan: Reforms of the Tanzimat Era
7. Q: What were the Tanzimat reforms, and why are they important to the chapter?
A: Show answer
The Tanzimat was a broad program of Ottoman reform that reshaped administration, law, education and the relationship between the central government and provincial society. Khalidi links the reforms to a more centralized and interventionist state, new legal institutions, expanded schooling and a larger bureaucracy. These changes were intended to strengthen the empire and make its administration more systematic, but they also altered local power relationships. In Jerusalem, the effects were visible in the courts, schools, careers of educated officials and the changing prestige of religious and secular learning. The chapter treats the Tanzimat as a structural transformation whose consequences reached into everyday social and cultural life.
Study instruction:
- "List the main fields affected by the Tanzimat in the chapter and explain one practical consequence in Jerusalem for each field."
Key point:
- The Tanzimat changed state capacity and provincial institutions.
- Its effects were administrative, legal, educational and cultural.
Source links:
- Koçak and Sancaktutan: Reforms of the Tanzimat Era
8. Q: How did Ottoman centralization alter the relationship between the state and local communities?
A: Show answer
The expanding Ottoman bureaucracy and new legal and educational institutions increased the central government’s ability to reach provincial towns and districts. Areas that had previously relied more heavily on local, tribal, sectarian or family authorities came under more systematic administrative oversight. This changed who could make decisions, enforce rules and pursue careers in public service. Local elites did not simply disappear; many adapted to the new system and used their resources and connections to preserve influence. The result was a changing balance between imperial officials, local notables and community institutions. Khalidi’s analysis shows that centralization can transform local power without eliminating local agency.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the sources of authority available to a local notable before and after the expansion of the Ottoman bureaucracy."
Key point:
- Centralization increased the state’s reach into provincial society.
- Local elites often adapted rather than simply losing all influence.
Source links:
- Koçak and Sancaktutan: Reforms of the Tanzimat Era
9. Q: How did the new courts change the role of the shari‘a courts in Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that new courts administering laws influenced partly by Western legal models took over many functions previously handled by shari‘a courts. The older courts were gradually restricted to a narrower range of matters, especially aspects of personal status and religious jurisdiction. This did not mean that Islamic law or religious scholars vanished. Rather, the legal system became more differentiated, with new state courts and legal personnel handling an expanding range of disputes. The change affected the careers and prestige of judges, the kinds of legal knowledge that carried authority and the relationship between religious institutions and the state. Khalidi presents this as part of a wider transformation of Ottoman governance.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a before-and-after diagram showing the functions of the shari‘a courts and the new state courts, using only distinctions supported by the chapter."
Key point:
- New state courts assumed many functions formerly handled by shari‘a courts.
- The reform narrowed and reorganized religious jurisdiction rather than abolishing it entirely.
Source links:
- Koçak and Sancaktutan: Reforms of the Tanzimat Era
10. Q: Why did the growth of the Ottoman bureaucracy change career opportunities for Jerusalem’s educated elite?
A: Show answer
A larger and more specialized bureaucracy required officials who could work with new laws, administrative procedures, languages and forms of record keeping. Modernized schools supplied some of this personnel. Careers that had once depended heavily on religious scholarship and service in the Islamic learned hierarchy increasingly competed with opportunities in secular administration, education and other state institutions. Members of established families were often well placed to make the transition because they had wealth, education and experience in public life. The chapter therefore connects institutional reform to changes in social prestige and professional opportunity, showing how new forms of knowledge could become sources of authority.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- The expanding bureaucracy created new routes to status and influence.
- Modern education became increasingly valuable for public careers.

11. Q: How did the social prestige of Islamic learning change during the late Ottoman period?
A: Show answer
Islamic learning continued to be important, and many educated provincial notables still entered religious institutions and the Ottoman religious bureaucracy. However, Khalidi argues that these institutions gradually ceased to be the main locus of political and administrative power. As Western-influenced schools and state offices expanded, knowledge of modern subjects and foreign languages became increasingly valuable. The result was a change in the relative status of different kinds of learning, not the disappearance of Islamic scholarship. Family libraries continued to preserve and produce religious, historical and literary works, demonstrating that older intellectual traditions remained active even as new professional and educational systems developed.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between the continued existence of Islamic scholarship and the declining administrative prestige of the religious bureaucracy."
Key point:
- Islamic scholarship persisted while its institutional position changed.
- New forms of education altered the hierarchy of professional prestige.

- Hafida Ben Dahman: Educational Activities in the District of Jerusalem, 1899
12. Q: How did European intellectual and political ascendancy affect Ottoman provincial elites?
A: Show answer
As European states gained greater economic, military and diplomatic power over the Ottoman Empire, provincial elites could no longer assume that traditional Islamic learning alone represented the highest form of knowledge or political influence. Some notables increasingly valued Western languages, scientific and technical subjects, modern social thought and European-style education. Khalidi connects this shift to the wider change in the balance of power between Europe and the Ottoman state. The process was selective: elites could adopt particular methods or ideas while retaining religious commitments and local loyalties. The chapter therefore avoids describing Western influence as a simple or complete cultural conversion.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the external changes that increased the prestige of European knowledge and explain how local elites responded selectively."
Key point:
- European political ascendancy affected intellectual hierarchies in Ottoman society.
- Local elites adopted new knowledge in varied ways rather than uniformly abandoning older traditions.

13. Q: Why were Jerusalem’s notable families able to adapt to institutional change?
A: Show answer
Khalidi emphasizes the material resources, social connections, education and political experience of established families. These advantages helped them move from older institutions into the expanding state system while preserving much of their influence. Adaptation could involve sending family members to new schools, entering modern administrative or parliamentary roles, supporting libraries and publishing, and cultivating connections with reformist intellectuals. The chapter does not suggest that every family adapted equally or that older elite power remained unchanged. It shows how social capital could be converted from one institutional setting into another, allowing some families to remain influential during a major transformation of government and culture.

14. Q: Why are family libraries important sources for understanding late Ottoman cultural life?
A: Show answer
Family libraries preserve evidence of what educated households read, collected, copied, translated and considered worth keeping. Their holdings can reveal the coexistence of manuscript scholarship, printed editions of classical texts, European-language works, religious reform literature and modern scientific or social thought. Khalidi uses Jerusalem’s collections to show that intellectual change was not confined to formal schools or government offices. Libraries also reveal networks of exchange among scholars, reformers and notable families. They are not a complete record of what the whole population read, because access and literacy were unequal, but they provide unusually rich evidence about the intellectual world of the urban elite.

15. Q: What made the Khalidi Library a significant cultural institution in Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
The Khalidi Library was established by Hajj Raghib al-Khalidi in 1899 or 1900, drawing on family collections that had accumulated over generations. It was intended to be accessible to the public and to encourage learning, including both the revival of classical Islamic scholarship and engagement with modern subjects. Its holdings preserve manuscripts, printed books and family papers that illuminate the intellectual and social history of Jerusalem. Khalidi uses the library to show how a prominent family could turn private resources into a civic cultural institution. Its significance lies not only in the quantity of books but also in the purpose of making knowledge available beyond the household.

Key point:
- The Khalidi Library linked family scholarship with a public educational purpose.

A: Show answer
Comparing the libraries helps historians distinguish individual family interests from wider patterns of reading and collecting in Jerusalem. Khalidi notes that several collections held overlapping religious, historical and literary manuscripts as well as printed works and periodicals. Similarities suggest shared intellectual networks and common interests, while differences may reflect the histories and priorities of particular families or institutions. The comparison also helps test whether a collection is exceptional or representative of broader cultural practices. These libraries are not statistically representative samples of the entire population, but their combined evidence can reveal a richer picture than any one collection alone.

17. Q: Why did the continued copying of manuscripts matter even after printed books became available?
A: Show answer
The continued copying of manuscripts shows that older scholarly practices remained active well into the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. Manuscript production could preserve texts, support teaching, transmit commentaries and maintain scholarly prestige. The availability of print did not immediately make manuscripts obsolete, especially where handwritten works had established authority or where local scholarly networks valued copying. Khalidi uses the coexistence of manuscript and printed material to demonstrate that cultural change was layered. A new technology can expand access and alter scholarly practice without instantly eliminating older forms of knowledge production.

18. Q: Why did members of the Khalidi family acquire printed editions of classical Islamic works they already owned in manuscript form?
A: Show answer
Khalidi suggests that printed editions offered advantages beyond simply supplying another copy. Modern editions could incorporate comparison among manuscripts, editorial corrections, introductions, notes and other forms of scholarship that made older texts easier to study critically. Owning both manuscript and printed versions therefore reflects an interest in combining traditional learning with newer scholarly methods. This practice is a concrete example of intellectual continuity and adaptation: the family did not abandon the classical canon, but used new publishing methods to deepen its engagement with it. The chapter presents print as a tool for renewed scholarship, not merely a replacement for handwritten books.

Khalidi describes a salafi tendency concerned with reviving Islam through renewed attention to foundational religious sources and reform of Muslim societies. It was linked to a desire to recover intellectual vitality while responding to contemporary political and cultural pressures. Salafi interests could coexist with engagement in modern scholarship and European intellectual currents. Such reading indicates that some Jerusalem intellectuals were engaging with new explanatory frameworks beyond traditional religious scholarship. This interest did not mean that every reader accepted European theories uncritically. It shows that the intellectual environment was expanding and that local scholars were selecting ideas they considered useful for understanding social change and the position of Arab societies. Historians should therefore examine how a text was read, translated, collected or discussed rather than assuming that its presence in a library proves complete agreement with its author.

21. Q: What intellectual gaps did Jerusalem journalists identify in their own society?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites a 1912 complaint by journalist Sa‘id Jarallah that Palestine had a distinguished past but lacked sufficient local historical writing and adequate library resources. His criticism points to a perceived gap between the region’s historical importance and the capacity of its contemporary institutions to document and interpret that history. Such complaints were not merely literary judgments; they carried public and patriotic purposes. Writers argued that a community needed its own libraries, scholars and historical narratives if it was to understand its past and participate confidently in modern intellectual life. These critiques are evidence of reformist concern, not neutral measurements of every library or school.

The founders presented libraries as institutions that could revive learning, broaden access to knowledge and strengthen the intellectual resources of society. The announcement of its foundation linked libraries with a broader recovery of cultural and social vitality. Journalists later argued that Palestinians should write their own history rather than rely entirely on accounts produced by others. These ambitions show how cultural institutions could become part of a public project: they preserved knowledge, trained readers and provided resources for communities seeking to interpret their past and future.

23. Q: Why was access to books and scholarship socially unequal in late Ottoman Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
Khalidi stresses that books and formal scholarship remained concentrated among a relatively small educated elite while much of the population was illiterate. Family libraries and intellectual circles therefore reveal important cultural activity but cannot be assumed to represent the reading habits of the entire society. The growth of schools and newspapers gradually widened access, but the transition was incomplete and uneven. This distinction matters because historians may overestimate the reach of elite ideas if they infer mass influence solely from the existence of a library or the publication of a newspaper. The chapter asks researchers to examine the channels through which knowledge reached people beyond the traditional scholarly class.

24. Q: How did the expansion of education affect social change in Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
New schools taught foreign languages, modern science, mathematics and other subjects alongside some traditional learning. They reached beyond the narrow circles served by older scholarly institutions and helped prepare students for new careers in administration, commerce, teaching and public life. Education also changed family expectations and created new forms of social mobility. Khalidi emphasizes that the demand for schooling came from within Palestinian society as well as from Ottoman reform and foreign missionary activity. The growth of education should therefore be understood as both a state project and a response to local aspirations for opportunity, knowledge and participation in a changing world.

25. Q: What do the chapter’s school statistics reveal about educational opportunity in Jerusalem?

Khalidi compares Ottoman yearbook figures from 1871 with later information about Christian, Jewish and missionary schools and with figures for public schools established by 1914. The figures suggest growth in educational provision, but the sources are not directly comparable because they count different institutions, populations and periods. The chapter also notes that the number of places remained insufficient to meet demand. The statistics therefore show both expansion and continuing limits. They should not be used to rank communities simplistically or to infer exact literacy rates without checking definitions, coverage and the reliability of each source.

26. Q: Why did the growth of state schools not eliminate private and missionary education?
A: Show answer
The Ottoman state expanded its own school system, but local demand for modern education exceeded available places and families had different educational preferences. Christian communities, foreign missions and private Muslim initiatives operated schools with distinct curricula, languages and institutional sponsors. Some offered subjects or opportunities that state schools did not provide at the same scale. Khalidi argues that the result was a diverse educational environment rather than a single unified system. This diversity increased educational opportunities for some students while also creating competition over language, loyalty, religious instruction and cultural influence.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a state school, a private Muslim school and a missionary school using sponsor, curriculum, language, intended students and political context."
Key point:
- State expansion coexisted with private, communal and missionary education.
- A diverse school system created opportunity but also competing educational influences.

27. Q: What was the significance of Rawdat al-Ma‘arif in Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Rawdat al-Ma‘arif as a modern Muslim private school established to meet growing demand for education that included sciences, mathematics and other modern subjects. It represented an effort by local educators and notables to provide a curriculum that could prepare students for contemporary public and professional life while remaining rooted in the society that supported it. The school illustrates that educational modernization was not simply imported by foreign missionaries or imposed by the Ottoman state. Local Muslim initiatives also responded to changing needs and helped shape a new generation of educated Palestinians.

- "Identify the school’s educational purpose and explain how it demonstrates local initiative within a wider period of institutional reform."

- Local Muslim educators helped drive modern educational change.

28. Q: What was the role of Khalil al-Sakakini’s Constitutional School?
A: Show answer
Khalil al-Sakakini founded al-Madrasa al-Dusturiyya, or the Constitutional School, in 1909. Khalidi presents it as another Jerusalem initiative intended to provide a modern education in sciences, mathematics and related subjects. The school’s name and founder’s public role also connected education with the political and intellectual environment created by the restoration of the Ottoman Constitution in 1908. It illustrates how schools could be sites where new ideas about citizenship, public life and social improvement were encountered. The chapter treats such initiatives as part of a broader educational ferment rather than isolated experiments.

29. Q: How did Christian and missionary schools contribute to Jerusalem’s educational environment?
A: Show answer
Christian private and missionary schools expanded educational opportunities and introduced curricula, languages and teaching methods influenced by institutions abroad. Khalidi notes well-known examples such as St. George’s School and French Catholic schools, while also discussing the appeal of missionary education to families beyond the communities that founded it. These schools could provide access to modern subjects and foreign languages, but they were also part of a wider competition among the Ottoman state and foreign powers for cultural influence. Their impact should be analyzed through actual curricula, student populations and institutional aims rather than assumed from their religious sponsorship alone.

30. Q: Why did education become an arena of competition between the Ottoman state and foreign powers?
A: Show answer
Schools shaped language, historical knowledge, religious instruction and the skills of future officials and professionals. The Ottoman government sought to strengthen its authority and educate personnel for a more centralized state, while foreign missions and institutions also sought to cultivate influence through schooling. Local families and educators had their own reasons for choosing particular schools, including access to languages, employment and modern subjects. Khalidi describes this as a contest over the minds of future generations, but also emphasizes local demand. Education was therefore not merely a technical service; it was a field in which state-building, religious identity, social mobility and international influence intersected.

31. Q: What were the consequences of having several different educational systems operating in Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
The coexistence of state, private Muslim, Christian communal and foreign missionary schools created a fragmented educational landscape. Institutions could use different curricula, languages and forms of religious instruction, and they were controlled by different authorities. This diversity offered families choices and expanded educational provision, but it also meant that students did not necessarily receive a common educational experience. The system could reinforce differences in social networks, language proficiency and access to professional pathways. Khalidi argues that education remained an important source of continuity with older social structures while becoming a major driver of change and competition.

32. Q: What was the Nahda, and how did it relate to Jerusalem’s cultural life?
A: Show answer
The Nahda was a broad cultural and intellectual revival across Arabic-speaking regions in the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. It involved renewed literary production, translation, publishing, education, reform debates and engagement with new political and scientific ideas. Khalidi places Jerusalem within this wider movement while emphasizing the city’s particular institutions and networks. The Nahda was not a single organization or ideology; it involved people from different religious, social and intellectual backgrounds. Its significance for Jerusalem lay partly in the spread of books and periodicals, the growth of local publishing and the circulation of debates that connected the city to Beirut, Cairo, Damascus and other centers.

33. Q: Why does Khalidi describe the press as one of the most widespread influences on cultural life?
A: Show answer
Newspapers and periodicals could circulate information and ideas beyond the small circles with direct access to libraries and formal scholarship. They reported political events, serialized literature, discussed education and social reform, and gave public visibility to arguments about Palestine and the region. Copies could be posted in public places, passed from hand to hand or read aloud, allowing people with limited literacy to encounter their contents. The press was therefore not influential only through individual subscribers. It helped create a wider public sphere in which people could follow common issues and debate the direction of their society.

34. Q: How did newspapers connect Jerusalem with other intellectual centers in the Arab world?

Jerusalem newspapers and libraries were linked to publications from Beirut, Cairo, Damascus and Istanbul. Periodicals produced elsewhere were collected in local libraries, while serialized material and news could be republished in daily newspapers. These connections allowed readers and editors in Jerusalem to participate in wider discussions about literature, reform, science, religion and politics. The chapter treats this circulation as evidence that Jerusalem was not intellectually isolated. Its local press operated within a regional network of publishers, writers and readers, while also developing concerns specific to Palestine and its social conditions.

35. Q: What can the surviving holdings of newspapers and periodicals in Jerusalem libraries tell historians?
A: Show answer
Bound runs of newspapers and periodicals in family libraries reveal what some readers collected and preserved over time. The Khalidi and al-Aqsa libraries held publications from several intellectual centers, including journals concerned with literature, science, religion and public affairs. Multiple copies or long runs may indicate sustained interest, while ownership marks can help identify readers and networks. These holdings cannot establish the exact size or social composition of the readership, but they provide material evidence that periodicals circulated and were valued. They also allow historians to compare published debate with the intellectual interests visible in private collections.

36. Q: How did serialization help newspapers reach a broader audience?
A: Show answer
Serialization allowed material first published in longer journals or books to appear in installments in daily newspapers. This made literary and intellectual discussions available to readers who might not purchase specialized periodicals or have access to private libraries. Newspapers could combine serialized writing with current events, commentary and local news, bringing different kinds of material into the same public channel. Khalidi uses this practice to show how the press widened access to regional intellectual production. Serialization also encouraged repeated engagement because readers could follow a subject over several issues rather than encounter it only as a complete book.

37. Q: What does the history of Al-Quds reveal about journalism in Jerusalem after 1908?
A: Show answer
Al-Quds was founded in Jerusalem in 1908 by Jurji Hanania and became one of the city’s notable late Ottoman newspapers. The National Library of Israel’s archive records issues from September 1908 to December 1913 and describes it as a daily social, literary and political paper that promoted reform and a multi-party political system. The chapter places Al-Quds within a wider newspaper environment that included other titles with different editorial positions. Its history illustrates how the constitutional opening created opportunities for publishing, while the limited size of the market and changing political conditions made newspaper production difficult.

38. Q: Why should historians avoid assuming that every newspaper in Jerusalem had the same political position?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several newspapers with differing editorial approaches. Some frequently criticized Zionist activity, while others adopted a more muted tone or published occasional criticism without making it their dominant focus. Newspapers also differed in ownership, audience, language, financial resources and relationship with authorities. Treating the press as one unified voice would conceal these distinctions and overstate the degree of public consensus. Historians should examine individual issues, editorial statements and patterns over time, while recognizing that a newspaper’s position could change with events or editorial leadership.

39. Q: What factors limited the commercial viability of Jerusalem newspapers?
A: Show answer
Khalidi discusses complaints by editors about the small market for quality newspapers and the large number of illiterate people. Publishing required printing equipment, paper, writers, distribution and enough paying readers to sustain operations. Short-lived papers and changing numbers of titles indicate that the press was vulnerable to financial constraints as well as political pressure. Yet limited commercial viability did not mean that newspapers had no influence. Copies could be shared or read publicly, and editors could reach audiences beyond subscribers. The chapter therefore distinguishes the economic sustainability of a newspaper from its potential cultural and political reach.

40. Q: How did newspapers circulate beyond their immediate subscribers?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that newspapers could be posted in public places, passed from hand to hand and arrive in distant towns or villages after a delay. In communities accustomed to receiving news slowly, a paper that arrived days after publication could still be eagerly read. Some editors recognized the potential rural audience and used distribution networks to reach it. Public reading and discussion could also transmit information to people who did not read the text themselves. These practices make it difficult to measure press influence solely through subscription or print-run statistics.

41. Q: What does government censorship or newspaper closure reveal about the influence of the press?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that authorities sometimes closed newspapers after they published material considered offensive or politically troublesome. Such actions indicate that officials regarded the press as capable of spreading ideas or mobilizing opinion in ways that could challenge authority. Censorship is not proof that every article had a mass audience, but it is evidence that publication was politically consequential enough to attract official attention. Historians should examine the specific grounds for closure, the authority responsible and the broader political circumstances rather than assume that every closure had the same cause.

A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that late Ottoman newspapers increasingly referred to Palestine as a country and to Palestinians as a population with shared concerns. Titles such as Filastin made the territorial focus explicit, while editorials and articles discussed local history, education and political conditions. This language helped make Palestine visible as a subject of public debate. The press did not invent every attachment to the land; rather, it provided a modern medium through which older local and religious connections could be expressed in a more explicitly territorial and political vocabulary. The chapter treats these usages as evidence of an evolving public consciousness.

43. Q: Why did some journalists call for Palestinians to write their own history?
A: Show answer
Journalists argued that a society needed to document and interpret its own past rather than depend entirely on narratives produced by outsiders. Khalidi connects this appeal to the perceived weakness of local libraries and historical writing, and to the broader purpose of cultural institutions in restoring intellectual confidence. Writing local history could preserve knowledge about communities, places and political developments that might otherwise be neglected or interpreted through external priorities. The call was both scholarly and civic: it treated historical knowledge as a resource for understanding the present and shaping future public life.

A newspaper could support Ottoman political rights, participate in the Arabic Nahda and focus its reporting on Palestine without seeing these commitments as mutually exclusive. The chapter therefore rejects the assumption that a person had to choose one exclusive identity at every moment. Their activities can be followed partly through newspaper coverage, which shows how associations contributed to urban public life. These groups were not necessarily identical in membership or purpose: some were organized around political ideas, others around religion, education or social interaction. Together they widened the institutional landscape through which people participated in cultural and political change.

Khalidi treats these actors as part of a wider international environment that shaped local education and cultural life. Their influence was not uniform: some contributed to scholarship or preservation, while institutions could also serve political or religious agendas. 48. Q: How did transportation, communications and improved security affect cultural life in late Ottoman Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi links improved security, population growth, transport and communications to economic expansion and a more dynamic social environment. Railways and port connections strengthened Jaffa and Haifa, while better movement of people and information connected towns and villages. These developments helped students, books, newspapers, officials and visitors circulate more widely. They also created opportunities for new occupations and commercial activity. The effects were uneven across regions, but the general result was a society in transition, with more channels for exchange and new pressures on traditional institutions. Infrastructure therefore mattered to cultural change as well as to trade.

49. Q: How did the changing economy affect the social structure of Jerusalem and Palestine?

The chapter associates economic expansion with population growth, improved security, transportation and new commercial opportunities. As the economy diversified, more people from different backgrounds could enter professions and public life. The old notable families remained influential, but they no longer monopolized cultural, political and administrative activity to the same degree. Hundreds of educated individuals increasingly participated in public affairs, while new occupations opened routes to prosperity beyond inherited elite status. The result was a more socially dynamic environment in which education and communication became important sources of mobility and influence.

50. Q: How did the role of traditional notable families change during the late Ottoman period?
A: Show answer
Notable families retained influence because of their wealth, property, educational resources and long-standing connections, but the institutional environment around them changed. New schools, bureaucratic careers, newspapers and associations allowed a wider group of educated individuals to enter public life. Khalidi argues that family rivalries and elite competition became less decisive as culture, politics and government were no longer the exclusive preserve of a small set of families. This did not mean the old elite vanished; rather, its members had to adapt and share influence with people whose authority came from modern education, professional work and public communication.

51. Q: What does the chapter suggest about the relationship between cultural change and political identity?
A: Show answer
Cultural institutions shaped political identity by determining which languages, histories and ideas circulated through society. Schools introduced new subjects and trained future officials; libraries preserved and reinterpreted older knowledge; newspapers connected readers through common debates; clubs and associations provided spaces for political discussion. These institutions did not all promote the same ideology, and they could be controlled by different authorities. Their combined effect was to expand the public sphere and provide new ways for people to think about Palestine, the Ottoman Empire, Arab society and the wider world. Cultural change therefore helped create the conditions in which modern political identities could be articulated.

52. Q: Why is it misleading to describe late Ottoman Jerusalem as either wholly traditional or fully Westernized?
A: Show answer
The evidence in the chapter shows traditional religious scholarship, manuscript copying, family patronage and local notables continuing alongside modern schools, state courts, foreign-language learning, printed books and European intellectual influences. People could use a new printed edition of a classical text, study modern science and remain committed to Islamic scholarship. Families could enter new administrative institutions while preserving older networks of authority. The city was therefore neither frozen in a traditional past nor transformed into a replica of a European city. Khalidi’s analysis emphasizes selective adoption, institutional overlap and different rates of change across social groups.

53. Q: How should historians use school records, library catalogues and newspaper archives together?
A: Show answer
Each source type answers different questions. School records can show institutional provision, enrollment and curricula, but their statistics may use inconsistent categories. Library catalogues reveal what collections preserved and valued, but not necessarily who read every item. Newspaper archives show public language, editorial positions and publication patterns, but cannot alone establish the views of all readers. Combining the sources allows historians to compare formal institutions with private collecting and public debate. It also makes it possible to identify gaps between official policy, elite activity and broader social access. Triangulation is essential because no single source provides a complete picture of cultural life.
Study instruction:
- "For one claim about education or public opinion, identify which source type would provide direct evidence and which would provide only indirect evidence."
Key point:
- Different sources have different evidentiary strengths and limitations.
- Triangulation produces a more reliable account than relying on one archive.

54. Q: What methodological lesson does the chapter offer about studying cultural change in late Ottoman Palestine?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates the value of studying institutions, texts, social networks and material conditions together. Changes in education cannot be understood without examining state centralization and local demand; libraries must be read alongside print culture and elite networks; newspapers must be considered in relation to literacy, distribution and censorship. Jerusalem’s influence must be connected to other Palestinian cities and wider regional networks. Khalidi’s approach also resists a simple story of Westernization by showing how older traditions adapted and remained active. The broader lesson is to explain cultural change as a process involving multiple actors, unequal access, continuity and contestation.

- "Prepare an outline that links government reform, education, libraries, the press, associations and economic change into one causal account of late Ottoman cultural life."
Key point:
- Cultural change is best explained through interacting institutions and social processes.
- Historical analysis must account for unequal access, continuity and competing agendas.
