Table 5
Peasant Resistance, Land Dispossession, and Palestinian Identity
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 5, “Peasant Resistance to Zionist Settlement”?
A: Show answer
Rashid Khalidi argues that rural Palestinian resistance to land sales, displacement, and the replacement of Arab cultivators was not a marginal or merely local phenomenon. It helped make Zionism a central public issue before 1914 and contributed to a shared sense of identity linking village communities with urban intellectuals and political figures. The chapter traces this process through land tenure changes, early clashes, the al-Fula affair, and the press and parliamentary debates that amplified rural grievances. It presents peasant resistance as one important strand in the formation of Palestinian identity, not as its sole cause.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter’s argument in a chain connecting land transactions, peasant experience, public advocacy, and collective identity."
Key point:
- Peasant resistance helped connect local land conflicts to wider political consciousness.
- The chapter treats identity formation as a process shaped by interaction between rural and urban society.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
2. Q: Why does Khalidi begin with the problem of who gets to write history?
A: Show answer
The chapter opens by noting that surviving historical records are disproportionately produced by people and institutions with literacy, power, and secure archives. As a result, the actions and perspectives of rural cultivators can be less visible than those of governments, landowners, settlers, and urban elites. This imbalance does not prove that the peasantry was politically inactive; it warns historians that the documentary record is uneven. Khalidi therefore asks readers to examine how source availability shapes historical interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the events that occurred from the events that surviving written records make easiest to see."
Key point:
- The written archive is not a neutral or complete mirror of society.
- Historical visibility often reflects power and literacy, not the relative importance of a group.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
3. Q: How did the disruption of Palestinian research institutions affect historical scholarship?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes how war, occupation, displacement, closures, and institutional instability damaged Palestinian archives, universities, and research organizations. He notes the seizure and later return of the Palestine Research Center’s historical archives after the 1982 invasion of Lebanon, the disruption of academic institutions in Beirut, and constraints affecting West Bank and Gaza universities. These conditions limited continuity in research and access to records. The chapter uses this history to explain why the evidence base for Palestinian rural history is fragmented, rather than treating gaps as proof that relevant events did not happen.
Study instruction:
- "When assessing a historical claim, identify which archives survived, who could access them, and what institutional disruptions shaped the record."
Key point:
- Archival loss and restricted access can distort what historians are able to reconstruct.
- Absence of surviving documentation is not equivalent to evidence of absence.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
4. Q: Why does the chapter call for greater use of Arabic and Ottoman sources?
A: Show answer
Khalidi criticizes accounts of Palestine that rely heavily on European travel writing, Zionist records, or Western sources while omitting Arabic and Ottoman materials. Such selection can leave local actors appearing only through outsiders’ descriptions. Arabic newspapers, Ottoman administrative records, local manuscripts, and village-level evidence can reveal how residents understood land, authority, and political change. The chapter does not reject Western or Zionist records; it argues that they should be read alongside indigenous sources and evaluated for perspective and limitations.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the source base of a historical account and note whose language, records, and testimony are missing."
Key point:
- Source diversity makes it easier to reconstruct local perspectives and contest one-sided narratives.
- No single archive or national historiography should be treated as sufficient by itself.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
5. Q: What is the historiographical problem with focusing mainly on urban elites?
A: Show answer
Urban politicians, journalists, merchants, and educated families left more written evidence and were more visible in formal politics. Historians therefore often gave them greater attention than rural cultivators, whose experiences were less frequently recorded. Khalidi argues that this can misrepresent the origins and social reach of opposition to Zionist settlement: urban debate was important, but many of its concerns were rooted in village-level experiences of land transfer and displacement. A fuller account must examine how rural grievances were transmitted, interpreted, and taken up by urban actors.
Study instruction:
- "Map the relationship between village-level events and the urban institutions that recorded or publicized them."
Key point:
- Greater documentary visibility does not mean that urban elites were the only politically significant actors.
- The chapter treats rural experience as a foundation for understanding wider political debate.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
6. Q: How can historians study peasant resistance when peasants left few written records?
A: Show answer
Khalidi proposes combining indirect and nontraditional evidence: land records, court and administrative documents, correspondence, newspapers, settlement-agency files, memoirs, and accounts written by officials or purchasers. These sources can reveal disputes over cultivation, eviction, compensation, boundaries, labor, and security even when the cultivators’ own testimony is missing. The method requires caution because many surviving descriptions were produced by people who opposed or misunderstood the peasants. Historians must compare accounts, identify the author’s position, and distinguish documented events from inferred motives.
Study instruction:
- "For a village dispute, list the available records, the likely interests of each record-maker, and the questions the evidence cannot answer."
Key point:
- Indirect evidence can recover aspects of marginalized groups’ experiences, but it must be critically evaluated.
- Attribution and uncertainty should be explicit when the participants’ own voices are unavailable.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
7. Q: What does “history from below” contribute to this chapter’s analysis?
A: Show answer
A history-from-below approach shifts attention from leaders and formal institutions to ordinary people and their lived relationships with land, work, and authority. Khalidi uses it not as an end in itself but to explain how local encounters helped shape the wider conflict and Palestinian identity. Village-level disputes show that political questions were experienced through practical matters such as the right to cultivate, remain in a home, graze animals, or retain a livelihood. The approach connects social history with political history without assuming that every local action was part of a fully articulated national program.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a local economic or social grievance can acquire wider political significance without assuming that every participant shared the same ideology."
Key point:
- Local experience can help explain the origins and social reach of political mobilization.
- A historian should not project later national language onto every earlier village action.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
8. Q: Was Arab awareness of Zionism present in Palestine before the British Mandate?
A: Show answer
Khalidi rejects the claim that Arab opposition to Zionism began only during the Mandate. He describes extensive discussion in Arabic newspapers and political circles before the First World War, especially after the restoration of the Ottoman Constitution in 1908. The issue appeared in local politics, Ottoman parliamentary debate, and press commentary across Palestine and other Arab regions of the empire. The chapter distinguishes this early awareness from later forms of organized nationalism: the existence of concern before 1914 does not mean that Palestinian identity was already uniform or fully consolidated.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the chronology of awareness and opposition from the later development of more organized national institutions and movements."
Key point:
- The chapter documents pre-1914 debate rather than locating all opposition in the Mandate period.
- Political consciousness developed over time and should not be treated as static.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
9. Q: What role did the Second Aliyah play in intensifying rural tensions?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies the Second Aliyah, especially the years from about 1904 or 1905 to 1914, as a turning point. Many new immigrants arrived with stronger political Zionist commitments and ideas associated with the “conquest of labor” and “conquest of the soil.” In practice, these ideas encouraged replacing Arab agricultural workers with Jewish workers and creating settlements in which Jewish immigrants would cultivate the land. This made the consequences of land purchases more severe for existing cultivators than in many earlier colonies that continued to employ Arab labor.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the labor arrangements described for earlier colonies with the more exclusivist settlement practices associated with the Second Aliyah."
Key point:
- The chapter links changes in labor policy to a more complete displacement of Arab cultivators.
- Immigration, ideology, land acquisition, and employment practices interacted to intensify conflict.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
10. Q: How did the Ottoman constitutional changes of 1908 affect public debate?
A: Show answer
The restoration of the Ottoman Constitution in 1908 and the relaxation of press censorship widened opportunities for political discussion. Arabic newspapers could address Zionism more openly, while Arab deputies could raise concerns in the Ottoman Parliament. Khalidi presents this new political space as an important condition for turning local land disputes into public controversies across the Arab provinces. The constitutional opening did not itself resolve land conflicts or eliminate imperial tensions; it made previously constrained criticism more visible and politically consequential.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how a change in political institutions can alter the channels through which local grievances become public issues."
Key point:
- The post-1908 press and parliamentary environment amplified debate about Zionism.
- Political opportunity helped connect village events with wider Arab political discussion.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
11. Q: What was the Ottoman Land Code of 1858, and why does the chapter consider it important?
A: Show answer
The Ottoman Land Code of 1858 changed the formal framework for registering and administering land, but its implementation in Palestine was gradual and regionally uneven. Khalidi argues that in some lowland areas it facilitated registration of land in individual names, including names of people who were not the cultivators. This could separate formal title from long-standing customary use and make cultivators vulnerable when registered owners sold land. Later scholarship likewise emphasizes the code’s role in changing the relationship between peasants, non-cultivating owners, and land registration, while stressing the need to study local variations.
Study instruction:
- "Review the distinction between the law’s formal provisions and the different ways they were implemented in particular regions."
Key point:
- The 1858 Code mattered partly because registration could diverge from customary cultivation rights.
- Its effects should not be described as uniform or instantaneous across all of Palestine.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
12. Q: What do miri and musha‘ mean in the chapter’s discussion of land tenure?
A: Show answer
Miri refers to a category of land in which the state retained ultimate title while individuals held recognized rights of use or cultivation under the applicable legal framework. Musha‘ refers to communal or periodically shared arrangements of agricultural use found in some villages. These systems could protect customary access even when cultivators did not possess individual freehold deeds in the modern sense. Khalidi argues that changes in registration and the disregard of communal rights could undermine the practical security of cultivators. The terms should be understood within Ottoman legal and local customary contexts, not simply equated with modern private ownership.
Study instruction:
- "Define each term in its historical setting and explain why legal title, cultivation, and communal use may belong to different people."
Key point:
- Formal ownership and customary usufruct were not necessarily the same thing.
- Land-tenure categories varied and require attention to local practice and legal status.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
13. Q: Why did some peasants avoid registering land in their own names?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that some cultivators feared that registration could expose them to taxation, other state exactions, or conscription. In some areas, village shaykhs, tax collectors, and urban notables were better positioned to navigate administrative procedures and register land in their own names. The result could be a mismatch between the person recorded as owner and the family that had long cultivated the land. The chapter presents this as one mechanism through which customary rights became vulnerable, while recognizing that the process differed across regions and land categories.
Study instruction:
- "When studying a land record, ask why the named registrant may differ from the actual cultivator and what local obligations influenced registration decisions."
Key point:
- Non-registration could reflect rational responses to state burdens, not lack of interest in the land.
- Registration processes could shift power toward intermediaries with administrative and financial advantages.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
14. Q: How did the growth of urban commerce affect rural landholding?
A: Show answer
As trade, transport, security, and agricultural production expanded in the late nineteenth century, prosperous merchants in Beirut, Haifa, Jaffa, Gaza, and other cities invested in land. Some acquired large tracts in fertile lowlands and registered them in their own names, sometimes at the expense of cultivators or customary users. Land increasingly became a commercial asset for owners who might live far from the villages. Khalidi argues that this concentration of formal ownership helped make large-scale transactions possible when Zionist purchasing organizations entered the market.
Study instruction:
- "Follow the chain from expanding trade and merchant wealth to land investment, registration, sale, and the consequences for cultivators."
Key point:
- Land markets were shaped by broader economic change, not only by the later arrival of Zionist purchasers.
- Absentee ownership could separate decision-making about land from the people who worked it.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
15. Q: Why did land tenure change differently in the hills and lowlands?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the effects of the 1858 Code as uneven. In some hill regions, small plots and individual ownership or usufruct were already common, so the new framework appears to have had less effect on concentration and alienation. In fertile lowlands, communal musha‘ arrangements and state-land categories were more prevalent in some areas, and registration in individual names could more sharply affect customary claims. The chapter therefore cautions against treating Palestine as one uniform land-tenure system.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the historical tenure patterns of hill villages and fertile lowland areas before drawing general conclusions about land registration."
Key point:
- Regional differences shaped how legal change affected cultivators.
- A countrywide generalization can conceal important local variations.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
16. Q: Why were the coastal plain, Marj Ibn ‘Amir, and eastern Galilee important to settlement and land conflict?
A: Show answer
These areas contained fertile or potentially productive land and, in many places, large parcels whose formal ownership had become concentrated in relatively few hands. The coastal plain offered opportunities for citrus cultivation and export, while the Marj Ibn ‘Amir and eastern Galilee supported expanded grain cultivation as security improved. Zionist purchasing organizations were attracted to these regions because of their agricultural potential and the possibility of acquiring large tracts. Khalidi describes the resulting settlement pattern as an N-shaped corridor running along the coast, through the Jezreel Valley, and toward the eastern Galilee and Lake Tiberias.
Study instruction:
- "Locate the three regions on a map and explain how fertility, ownership concentration, transport, and settlement geography interacted."
Key point:
- Land suitability and ownership structure influenced where large purchases were concentrated.
- The geography of settlement had both agricultural and territorial significance.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
17. Q: How did citrus production connect Palestine’s countryside to the world economy?
A: Show answer
Khalidi uses the Jaffa orange trade to illustrate the growing integration of Palestine into international markets. Improvements in steam navigation and transport made export-oriented citrus cultivation more profitable, encouraging investment and drawing labor into coastal areas. By 1913, the chapter reports about 1.6 million cases of oranges shipped from Jaffa, valued at nearly £300,000, while much of the citrus land remained Arab-owned and cultivated. The example shows that the countryside was already changing economically before the major conflicts over land purchases intensified.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how transport, export demand, capital investment, and agricultural labor can transform a rural economy."
Key point:
- Palestinian agriculture was commercially connected to regional and global markets.
- Economic modernization was already underway before the events examined in the later case studies.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
18. Q: Why does the chapter warn that pre-1914 population figures are uncertain?
A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that there are no precise or fully reliable population totals for Palestine just before the First World War. He discusses competing estimates, including a total population above 720,000 and different estimates of the Jewish population, with the chapter favoring about 60,000 over a higher figure of 85,000 based on its reading of Ottoman and other data. Migration, departures, and differences in how Ottoman and foreign nationals were counted complicate the figures. These numbers should therefore be presented as estimates tied to particular sources and methods, not as exact census facts.
Study instruction:
- "When using historical population statistics, record the source, date, counting method, and reasons the estimate may be incomplete."
Key point:
- Population estimates are method-dependent and contested in the chapter.
- A precise-looking number should not conceal uncertainty in the underlying records.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
19. Q: How did a typical land-purchase and settlement sequence affect Arab cultivators?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a recurring sequence: a large parcel was purchased from an absentee owner; the new owner or agency asserted formal rights; existing cultivators were asked to leave or were reclassified as tenants or laborers; and new settlers then occupied or cultivated the land. In some cases, former cultivators retained access through leases or employment, but in others they were expelled. The consequences depended on the purchaser’s labor policy, the legal status of the land, the strength of customary claims, and the intervention of state authorities.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the sequence from sale to settlement and identify the decision point at which cultivators’ residence or use rights were lost."
Key point:
- A sale of formal title could alter the position of people who continued to live on and work the land.
- The outcome varied with the new owner’s intentions and the rights recognized in practice.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
20. Q: What is the difference between legal title and usufruct in the chapter’s account?
A: Show answer
Legal title concerns the formally recognized ownership or registered claim to land; usufruct concerns the right to use, cultivate, or benefit from it. A family could have cultivated a parcel for generations without holding the kind of registered title that a later purchaser or official recognized. Khalidi argues that peasants often regarded their customary use rights as secure and inalienable, even when a formal transfer of title occurred. Conflict arose when new owners treated the registration as sufficient to remove people whose livelihoods and homes depended on the land.
Study instruction:
- "Use a hypothetical village parcel to distinguish the registered owner, customary cultivator, tenant, and any communal rights-holder."
Key point:
- Formal transfer of title did not automatically settle every customary or practical claim.
- The distinction between ownership and use is essential to understanding the dispossession described.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
21. Q: Why did sales to Arab landlords and sales to Zionist agencies have different consequences for cultivators?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that both kinds of purchase could weaken cultivators’ claims to land, but the labor consequences often differed. An Arab landlord generally needed cultivators to continue working the property and could retain them as tenants. Some Zionist settlement organizations, especially as “conquest of labor” gained influence, sought to replace Arab workers with Jewish settlers and to create land that would be cultivated by Jewish labor. This distinction is presented as a tendency in the cases examined, not as a universal rule applying to every Arab owner or every Jewish settlement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the purchaser’s economic model and labor policy before concluding what a land sale meant for the people already cultivating the parcel."
Key point:
- The identity and objectives of the purchaser affected whether cultivators could remain as tenants or workers.
- The chapter distinguishes transfer of title from the further question of who would live and labor on the land.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
22. Q: How did the First Aliyah settlements differ from the practices associated with the Second Aliyah?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes many early settlements established during the First Aliyah, dated in the chapter to 1882–1903, as relatively pragmatic and less ideologically committed to excluding Arab labor. In a number of cases, Arab cultivators continued working as laborers or tenants after land changed hands, which could reduce immediate friction without restoring their former rights. The Second Aliyah brought stronger commitments to Jewish labor and cultivation, and greater willingness to use armed guards to defend newly acquired land. The chapter presents this shift as an important change in the character of settlement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare land ownership, labor policy, and security practices in the chapter’s accounts of the two immigration waves."
Key point:
- The chapter identifies a shift from partial incorporation of Arab labor toward more exclusive labor and settlement practices.
- Period labels are useful, but practices still varied among individual settlements.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
23. Q: What did “conquest of labor” mean in practice?
A: Show answer
In the chapter, “conquest of labor” refers to efforts to replace Arab workers with Jewish workers in agricultural and other settlement-related occupations. This was more than a slogan about employment: it affected who could earn wages on newly purchased land and whether former cultivators could remain economically connected to their villages. When combined with the goal of exclusive Jewish cultivation, it made displacement more complete than in colonies that continued employing Arab labor. The term should be explained in its historical context rather than treated as a generic description of ordinary labor competition.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the practical effects of a labor-exclusion policy on former cultivators, local wages, settlement organization, and relations between communities."
Key point:
- Labor policy was a central mechanism through which land acquisition changed village life.
- The chapter links the exclusion of Arab labor to the more exclusivist character of later settlement.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
24. Q: What did “conquest of the soil” imply for existing cultivators?
A: Show answer
The phrase expressed the objective of establishing Jewish cultivation and settlement on acquired land, not merely securing legal ownership. Where land was already cultivated, realizing that objective could require replacing existing Arab farmers and tenants with Jewish settlers. Khalidi argues that this intensified the conflict between the formal purchaser and the people who regarded themselves as having enduring rights to live on and work the land. The chapter treats land and labor as connected questions: control of the soil was also a question of who would inhabit it and earn a livelihood from it.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a change in cultivation policy can affect residence, employment, and community continuity as well as property ownership."
Key point:
- The chapter treats land acquisition and labor replacement as linked processes.
- The effects of settlement depended on how new owners intended to use the land.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
25. Q: How does the chapter challenge the slogan “A land without a people for a people without a land”?
A: Show answer
Khalidi contrasts the slogan with evidence that Palestine contained established villages, cultivated fields, export agriculture, and a substantial Arab population. He quotes Ahad Ha-Am’s 1891 observation that cultivated fields were widespread and that uncultivated land was not simply available for settlement without effort. The chapter argues that some supporters abroad imagined the country as empty, while visitors and officials who examined the land could see a more complex reality. The slogan is analyzed as a political representation that obscured the presence and rights of existing inhabitants.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a political slogan with contemporary descriptions, demographic estimates, agricultural evidence, and records of land use."
Key point:
- The chapter challenges an “empty land” narrative with evidence of existing cultivation and communities.
- Historical claims about land must be tested against local evidence rather than repeated as slogans.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
26. Q: What is the significance of Arthur Ruppin’s statement about removing peasants?
A: Show answer
Khalidi cites Ruppin, a senior Zionist land official, as recognizing that there was little arable unsettled land and that purchasing and settling land would require removing the peasants who cultivated it, including owners and tenants. The quotation is significant because it shows that the presence of existing cultivators was known within settlement leadership, not simply overlooked by distant supporters. It also illustrates the practical implications of exclusive settlement objectives. The quotation should be read in its historical context and alongside the broader documentary record, not used as a substitute for examining individual transactions.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze what the statement reveals about land availability, settlement objectives, and the anticipated treatment of cultivators."
Key point:
- The chapter uses Ruppin’s words to show awareness of the human consequences of land acquisition.
- A quotation from one official is evidence of a perspective, not proof that every purchaser acted identically.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
27. Q: What happened in the Petah Tiqva conflict of 1886?
A: Show answer
Khalidi recounts a clash involving peasants from the neighboring village of Yahudiyya after land they considered theirs had been transferred to the colony. The dispute followed financial dealings involving Jaffa moneylenders and local authorities; the chapter notes evidence that some land may have been sold beyond what the vendors were entitled to convey and that tenant farmers may have held possession rights over part of the parcel. Ottoman troops intervened, arrests followed, and a Jewish settler was killed while others were wounded. The case illustrates that disputes over land and cultivation rights predated the Second Aliyah.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the transaction, the cultivators’ claim, the clash, and the state response without reducing the event to a single motive."
Key point:
- The Petah Tiqva incident is presented as an early example of resistance linked to disputed land rights.
- The chapter treats the case as part of a longer pattern, while acknowledging limits in the surviving evidence.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
28. Q: What do the early disputes at Gedera, Rehovot, Nes Ziyyona, and Hadera add to the argument?
A: Show answer
Khalidi cites several other late nineteenth-century disputes involving harassment, raids, and prolonged property claims. He notes that in some cases tensions diminished when former cultivators were able to lease land back or find permanent or seasonal work on parts of their former holdings. These cases help distinguish earlier arrangements, in which Arab labor was often retained, from later efforts to replace it. They also show that the history was not a simple progression from harmony to conflict; disagreements over ownership and use appeared early, with outcomes shaped by local arrangements.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the settlement cases and identify which factors eased or intensified conflict, especially tenancy and access to work."
Key point:
- Early land disputes existed before the more exclusivist practices of the twentieth century.
- Retaining cultivators as tenants or workers could reduce immediate conflict without restoring ownership.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
29. Q: What was the Tiberias-region land transaction involving the Jewish Colonization Association?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the Jewish Colonization Association’s efforts beginning in 1901 to remove cultivators from roughly 70,000 dunums in the Tiberias district, purchased mainly from the Sursuq family and its business partners. The affected communities included al-Shajara, Misha, and Malhamiyya, with neighboring villages also losing land. The JCA’s agent and the former owners pressed for eviction, while cultivators resisted and some were arrested after state intervention. Between 1901 and 1904, settlements including Sejera, Kfar Tavor, Yavniel, Menahamia, and Bet Gan were established on the acquired lands.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the sellers, purchasing body, affected villages, and state actions in the Tiberias case."
Key point:
- The Tiberias transaction linked absentee ownership, large-scale purchase, and the removal of village cultivators.
- The chapter emphasizes the role of state power in enabling the transfer to be implemented.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
30. Q: Why did the cultivators resist the Tiberias-region evictions?
A: Show answer
The cultivators had long worked the land and regarded their claims as grounded in residence, cultivation, and customary use. From their perspective, a transaction between absentee landlords and a new organization did not automatically extinguish those rights. The JCA’s intention to remove them and establish new settlements threatened homes, livelihoods, and continuity with village land. Khalidi also notes that the purchasers were perceived as outsiders with different political objectives, and that their claims were supported by an imperial administration that some residents increasingly viewed as distant or unresponsive.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the cultivators’ position from the perspective of livelihood, customary rights, community ties, and the process by which the land was transferred."
Key point:
- Resistance was tied to concrete claims over homes and cultivation, not only abstract political slogans.
- The identity and intentions of the new owners influenced how the sale was perceived.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- Amos Nadan: The Route from Informal Peasant Landownership to Formal Tenancy and Eviction
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
31. Q: What role did Amir Amin Arslan play in the Tiberias dispute?
A: Show answer
Amir Amin Arslan, the Arab district governor of Tiberias, opposed the land transaction and what he described as the de-Arabization of the district. Khalidi notes that he resisted the transfer on nationalist grounds, despite pressure from his superior, the governor of Beirut, who ultimately enforced the legal transfer. Arslan could not prevent the purchasers from taking possession, but he pressed for compensation for evicted tenants and, according to the account discussed by Khalidi, may have encouraged local resistance. His later election to the Ottoman Parliament connected local experience with wider political debate.
Study instruction:
- "Separate Arslan’s administrative actions, political arguments, and the limits imposed by his position in the Ottoman hierarchy."
Key point:
- Arslan acted as an intermediary between local grievances and wider Arab political opposition.
- His efforts did not reverse the transaction, illustrating the limits of local officials under central authority.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
32. Q: How did the 1909 clashes near al-Shajara affect the conflict?
A: Show answer
After the 1908 constitutional restoration, disputes that had persisted for years re-emerged more openly. In April 1909, former cultivators challenged boundaries and clashes near al-Shajara left two Arabs and two Jews dead, with others wounded; the chapter notes that eleven Arabs were arrested. The violence helped prompt a change in settlement security arrangements, including the public establishment of Ha-Shomer in April 1909. Khalidi interprets the incident as an escalation of earlier disputes rather than an isolated outbreak unrelated to land acquisition.
Study instruction:
- "Record the sequence from earlier land disputes to the 1909 clash, the casualties and arrests reported by the chapter, and the security response."
Key point:
- The 1909 incidents were connected to unresolved land and boundary disputes.
- The aftermath helped shape organized armed guarding in the new settlements.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
33. Q: What were Bar Giora and Ha-Shomer, and why are they discussed together?
A: Show answer
Bar Giora was a secret organization founded in 1907; in April 1909, a public paramilitary organization, Ha-Shomer (“the Guardian”), was established to guard Jewish agricultural settlements in Galilee. Khalidi describes Ha-Shomer as emerging from a milieu that emphasized Hebrew labor, settlement, and armed defense. The organization’s formation is linked to escalating clashes over land and to settlers’ desire to protect newly acquired property against people who continued to claim rights there. Later accounts describe Ha-Shomer as an institutional precursor to the Haganah and, ultimately, Israeli military structures.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the relationship between the earlier secret group, the public guarding organization, the land disputes, and later institutional narratives."
Key point:
- Ha-Shomer’s formation reflected a new organized approach to settlement security.
- The chapter connects its origins to the defense of newly acquired land amid continuing peasant claims.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
34. Q: Why did the replacement of Arab watchmen with Jewish guards matter?
A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that Jewish immigrants increasingly took over guarding roles previously performed by Arab watchmen, including at Sejera. This change removed a source of income for local workers and placed the armed defense of the settlements in the hands of people committed to the new settlement project. The guards were therefore defending land against former cultivators who still believed they had rights to it. The chapter treats this shift as a small-scale illustration of the wider conflict: ownership, labor, security, and political objectives were becoming intertwined.
Study instruction:
- "Consider how changing who performs security work can alter livelihoods, power relations, and the way a land dispute is enforced."
Key point:
- The change in guarding roles was both an employment issue and a security transformation.
- The chapter views settlement defense as part of the wider struggle over land and labor.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
35. Q: Why does Khalidi connect early peasant resistance with later Palestinian resistance movements?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that later Palestinian movements referred back to early rural resistance as a precedent and used symbols such as the kaffiyya and the language of muqawama, or resistance, to express continuity. Khalidi cautions that the names and motivations of many early participants are unknown, and that later movements constructed a narrative from fragmentary evidence. The point is not that every early clash was identical to later organized movements, but that later political actors interpreted these incidents as part of a longer history of defending land and community.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the events of the early twentieth century from the later ways in which political movements remembered and represented them."
Key point:
- Later movements used early peasant resistance as part of a historical narrative of continuity.
- Retrospective symbolism should not be mistaken for complete evidence about earlier participants’ intentions.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
36. Q: What made the al-Fula affair of 1910–11 especially consequential?
A: Show answer
The al-Fula transaction involved village land in the fertile Marj Ibn ‘Amir sold by Elias Sursuq to the Jewish National Fund. The former cultivators resisted, and the district governor Shukri al-‘Asali refused to facilitate the transfer, while the Beirut governor ultimately overruled him. The affair became widely discussed in Arabic newspapers and Ottoman political circles. Khalidi argues that its significance exceeded the size of the parcel because it made the consequences of land sales and peasant dispossession publicly visible and politically resonant across bilad al-Sham.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the political impact of a land transaction can be greater than its physical size or immediate local consequences."
Key point:
- Al-Fula became a public symbol of the wider issue of peasant dispossession.
- Its political reach came through local resistance, official intervention, journalism, and parliamentary debate.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
37. Q: Who was Shukri al-‘Asali, and how did he respond to the al-Fula sale?
A: Show answer
Shukri al-‘Asali was the Arab district governor of Nazareth, a member of a prominent Damascus family, and an experienced journalist and public speaker. After learning of the sale, he refused to hand over the title deed to the new owners despite instructions from his superior. He then published critical articles under the pseudonym “Salah al-Din al-Ayyubi” in the Damascus newspaper al-Muqtabas. Khalidi presents al-‘Asali as a figure who used the new constitutional and press environment to turn a local dispute into a broader political challenge to Zionist settlement and the Ottoman authorities’ handling of it.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the administrative action, public-writing strategy, and political opportunity that shaped al-‘Asali’s response."
Key point:
- Al-‘Asali linked a village land dispute to wider political and patriotic arguments.
- His actions illustrate how local officials and journalists could amplify rural grievances.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
38. Q: How did the Arabic press amplify the al-Fula affair?
A: Show answer
Articles about the transaction appeared in newspapers including al-Muqtabas, al-Karmil, al-Mufid, al-Ittihad al-‘Uthmani, and al-Haqiqa. They circulated beyond Nazareth and helped frame the expulsion of cultivators as a matter affecting the wider Arab public. Khalidi emphasizes that the press connected the peasants’ claims to questions of land, foreign settlement, loyalty to the empire, and the conduct of officials. Newspapers did not simply report an event; they selected facts, interpreted them, and helped make the affair a cause célèbre.
Study instruction:
- "Track how a local event moved through multiple newspapers and identify the arguments used to make it relevant to readers beyond the village."
Key point:
- The press transformed a local conflict into a wider public controversy.
- Circulation and framing helped connect rural grievances with regional political debates.

- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
39. Q: What was the role of the Ottoman authorities in the al-Fula transaction?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes conflict between the local governor, who resisted the sale’s implementation, and higher Ottoman authorities who upheld the purchaser’s claim and allowed the settlement of Merhavia to proceed in January 1911. The authorities treated the issue largely as a question of the seller’s property rights and the legality of the transfer, while cultivators emphasized their residence and customary claims. Khalidi uses this contrast to show how a formally legal transaction could still produce a major social and political dispute. The account also reveals divisions within the administration rather than a single uniform state position.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the reasoning of local and central officials and distinguish formal property law from the broader social consequences of enforcing a sale."
Key point:
- State intervention was decisive in allowing the new owners to take possession.
- The dispute involved competing understandings of property, public interest, and the rights of cultivators.

40. Q: How did the al-Fula affair connect Zionism to debates about Ottoman governance?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the affair became a vehicle for criticism of the Committee of Union and Progress and what some Arab politicians saw as growing Turkish domination. Critics charged that the central government was neglecting Arab concerns while allowing Zionist settlement to proceed. Whether every allegation was justified is not treated as settled; the chapter emphasizes that these beliefs were politically influential. The case therefore connected a local land dispute to broader arguments about imperial representation, constitutional government, Arabism, and the distribution of political power.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the facts of the land transaction from the political interpretations that different groups attached to the Ottoman government’s response."
Key point:
- The affair became part of a wider debate about Arab interests within the Ottoman Empire.
- Political impact depends partly on how an event is interpreted and circulated, not only on the original transaction.

41. Q: What does the chapter show about the relationship between property rights and social justice?
A: Show answer
The al-Fula debate exposed a tension between a seller’s formal right to dispose of property and the claims of people whose homes and livelihoods depended on the land. One official defended the owner’s right to sell where the law permitted it; critics argued that government should consider the consequences for the village inhabitants and their customary use. Khalidi does not reduce the issue to a simple legal formula. He shows that the conflict involved different conceptions of ownership, public responsibility, and the legitimacy of removing cultivators.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze the difference between a legally recognized transaction and its consequences for residents, tenants, and customary rights-holders."
Key point:
- A lawful sale can still generate serious social and political disputes.
- Property rules and the protection of existing occupants are distinct questions that must be examined together.

- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity

42. Q: How did land disputes contribute to the development of a shared urban-rural Palestinian outlook?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that peasant resistance and urban opposition gradually reinforced one another. Cultivators experienced land sales and removal directly; journalists, politicians, and intellectuals interpreted these events as evidence of a wider threat. Their public advocacy, in turn, helped connect village communities to people in cities and to debates elsewhere in the Arab provinces. This did not erase differences of class, locality, or political outlook. Rather, it created a shared perception that rural and urban Palestinians could be affected by the same process of settlement and dispossession.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the two-way relationship between village resistance and urban political advocacy, noting both shared interests and continuing differences."
Key point:
- The chapter sees identity formation as interaction between social groups rather than a one-way process led only by elites.
- A common concern over land helped create a sense of shared fate.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
43. Q: What role did Filastin and al-Karmil play in connecting villages to public debate?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes Filastin’s effort to distribute its anti-Zionist newspaper to villages in the Jaffa hinterland so that rural readers would be informed about developments across the country. He also highlights Najib Nassar’s al-Karmil, which paid close attention to rural events and reported on peasant resistance. These newspapers helped translate local disputes into a broader political narrative and encouraged readers to see village-level events as part of a wider issue. The chapter also notes that newspaper accounts were shaped by editors’ perspectives and the limits of available information.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how newspaper distribution, editorial framing, and coverage of rural events can create connections between local communities and wider publics."
Key point:
- The press was an active participant in the formation of public opinion.
- Distribution beyond cities helped make rural land disputes visible in national and regional debates.

- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
44. Q: What does the chapter’s discussion of land-sale statistics reveal about who sold land before 1914?
A: Show answer
Khalidi compares Avraham Granott’s published table with a more detailed compilation from Ruhi al-Khalidi’s unpublished work and other contemporary material. Granott’s categories for purchases up to 1914 assign shares to large absentee landlords, resident landlords, various institutions or sources, and fellahin. The more detailed pre-1907 list identifies named vendors for 247,466 dunums and, within that sample, estimates 58 percent from non-Palestinian absentee landlords, 36 percent from Palestinian absentee landlords, and 6 percent from local landlords and cultivators. These are partial datasets, not a complete census of every sale.
Study instruction:
- "Record each dataset’s period, coverage, categories, and denominator before comparing percentages."
Key point:
- The chapter argues that absentee landlords accounted for a large share of documented sales.
- The figures describe incomplete samples and should not be generalized without qualification.

- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity

45. Q: Why does Khalidi emphasize non-Palestinian absentee landlords in the land-sale debate?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the available pre-1914 evidence indicates a substantial role for non-Palestinian absentee owners, including Beirut merchants and other outside interests. He challenges accounts that focus mainly on Palestinian notables and cultivators as sellers. The chapter does not claim that Palestinians never sold land; it acknowledges sales by Palestinian landlords and fellahin while arguing that the overall distribution was more complex. The point is to assess the proportions and the commercial context rather than assign collective blame to one group.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter’s argument with the categories and limitations of the underlying land-sale records."
Key point:
- The chapter challenges a simplified narrative that attributes most sales solely to Palestinian sellers.
- Seller identity, residence, parcel size, and the coverage of the dataset all matter.

46. Q: What are the limits of the land-sale statistics used in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter repeatedly notes that the available figures cover only portions of the total land acquired and depend on incomplete records. Granott’s table was based on partial Jewish Agency figures, while Ruhi al-Khalidi’s list covers identified vendors for a subset of sales through 1907. Categories such as “various sources” also combine different kinds of sellers. The figures can support a cautious argument about broad patterns, but they cannot provide a complete parcel-by-parcel account of every transaction or establish the motives of each seller.
Study instruction:
- "State what the statistics can show, what they cannot show, and which assumptions are required to extrapolate from a sample."
Key point:
- Incomplete land records limit certainty about totals and seller categories.
- Quantitative claims should be presented with their source base and uncertainty.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
47. Q: How did the al-Fula affair contribute to Arab political mobilization beyond Palestine?
A: Show answer
The affair was reported in newspapers in Damascus, Beirut, Haifa, and other cities and became part of parliamentary debate after al-‘Asali’s election. Its emotional and political force came from the visible image of cultivators resisting removal from their homes to make way for new settlers. Khalidi argues that this helped urban audiences understand the consequences of land purchases in a way that abstract debate about Zionism had not. The episode therefore linked a local struggle to political discussion across bilad al-Sham and the Ottoman Arab provinces.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the path from local resistance to newspaper coverage, election campaigning, and parliamentary debate."
Key point:
- The affair’s significance came from its ability to make peasant dispossession politically legible to wider audiences.
- Local conflicts could become regional symbols when public institutions and media amplified them.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
48. Q: What does the chapter say about the relationship between peasant resistance and later mobilization around ‘Izz al-Din al-Qassam?
A: Show answer
Khalidi connects the earlier pattern of rural dispossession and urban advocacy to the later public role of ‘Izz al-Din al-Qassam. The chapter notes that many of al-Qassam’s followers were former tenant farmers displaced by land purchases and labor-exclusion policies, and that his funeral in Haifa in 1935 became a major public demonstration. It links this event to the wider mobilization that preceded the 1936–39 revolt. The author presents this as a historical continuity in social conditions and political memory, not as proof that the early village clashes and later organized resistance were identical.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish continuity in grievances and social networks from differences in organization, leadership, and historical circumstances."
Key point:
- Earlier rural dispossession formed part of the background to later mobilization.
- The chapter identifies connections without collapsing distinct periods into one movement.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
49. Q: How should the chapter’s account of violence be interpreted responsibly?
A: Show answer
Khalidi challenges descriptions that reduce all attacks on early settlements to ordinary criminality or banditry, arguing that many incidents were connected to dispossession and resistance to land transfer. At the same time, he acknowledges that banditry also occurred and that the evidence does not allow a complete reconstruction of every participant’s motives. The responsible approach is to examine the documented context of each incident, including land claims, employment changes, state intervention, and the accounts of different parties. Neither a blanket criminal label nor a blanket political explanation is sufficient.
Study instruction:
- "For each reported clash, separate documented actions, stated motives, inferred motives, and the perspective of the source describing it."
Key point:
- The chapter rejects one-dimensional explanations of violence while acknowledging evidentiary limits.
- Contextual analysis is stronger than assigning one motive to every incident.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
50. Q: Why does the chapter caution against treating the peasantry as politically or intellectually “non-existent”?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that illiteracy and limited direct documentation do not mean that peasants were unaware of political developments or incapable of collective action. They could understand events in their localities and respond when land sales threatened their homes, work, or customary rights. Their actions often became visible to wider audiences only when urban writers or officials recorded them. The chapter therefore distinguishes lack of written testimony from lack of awareness, agency, or political significance.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why literacy, access to print, and participation in formal institutions are incomplete measures of political awareness."
Key point:
- The rural population had agency even when it left few written records.
- Historians must avoid equating documentary silence with social or political passivity.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
51. Q: What are the main limitations that remain in reconstructing early peasant resistance?
A: Show answer
Khalidi acknowledges that many essential records have been lost, remain inaccessible, or were never created by rural participants. The surviving evidence often comes from Zionist, Ottoman, or Western sources whose authors had their own institutional interests and assumptions. Even when incidents can be documented, the identities and motivations of many peasants remain uncertain. The chapter therefore offers a reconstruction of broad patterns rather than a complete village-by-village narrative. Further research in Ottoman, British, Israeli, Palestinian, and other Arab archives could refine or challenge parts of the account.
Study instruction:
- "For each conclusion, distinguish direct documentation, corroboration from multiple sources, and the author’s reasoned inference."
Key point:
- The chapter’s conclusions are substantial but necessarily provisional in places.
- Archival limitations should shape the strength and wording of historical claims.
Source links:
- Columbia University Press: Palestinian Identity
- International Journal of Middle East Studies: Changing Capitalist Structures and Land Purchases in Northern Palestine
52. Q: What are the most important conclusions to retain from Chapter 5?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that land transactions, customary cultivation rights, labor-exclusion policies, and state enforcement created repeated conflicts in the Palestinian countryside before 1914. Peasant resistance made these consequences visible to urban intellectuals and politicians, while newspapers and parliamentary debate carried local grievances into wider Arab politics. The al-Shajara and al-Fula cases show how land disputes could become symbols of a broader struggle over settlement, labor, and political authority. Khalidi’s larger conclusion is that rural resistance and urban advocacy helped foster a shared Palestinian outlook, while the surviving evidence requires careful attention to bias and uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Write a synthesis that connects land tenure, labor policy, local resistance, the press, Ottoman politics, and the formation of shared identity."
Key point:
- Peasant resistance was a significant element in the development of Palestinian identity before 1914.
- The chapter’s argument depends on combining local history with critical source analysis.
