- The lack of a Palestinian state altered the means by which identity was transmitted.
- Multiple narratives could coexist without a single official national curriculum.
4. Q: Why does the chapter discuss the relationship between Palestinian and Israeli national narratives?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that public discussion of Palestinian history is often framed in relation to the much more widely recognized Israeli and Jewish historical narrative. In some settings, Palestinian voices are treated as legitimate only when accompanied by an Israeli counterpart, while the reverse expectation is not imposed as consistently. This can make Palestinian identity appear derivative or dependent on the other narrative. The chapter contends that Palestinians must be understood as a historical community with their own experiences and political development, even while recognizing that the two national narratives have become deeply intertwined and often contested.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a dominant public narrative can shape the conditions under which another community’s history is heard."
Key point:
- The chapter argues for recognizing Palestinian history as an independent subject of analysis.
- Narrative entanglement should not erase the distinct experiences and agency of Palestinians.
5. Q: What does the chapter mean when it says that identity is formed in relation to an “other”?
A: Show answer
Khalidi invokes the idea that self-definition often takes place through contrast with another group or narrative. The encounter with Zionism became one important context in which Palestinians understood their collective interests and political vulnerability. But the chapter rejects the claim that Zionism created Palestinian attachment to Palestine from nothing. Other external powers and governing authorities had also shaped local perceptions, including European imperial interests, Ottoman rule, and later British Mandatory administration. The “other” is therefore one factor in identity formation, not the sole origin or complete explanation of a community’s sense of itself.
Study instruction:
- "Identify several external actors that shaped Palestinian self-understanding at different moments and explain how their roles differed."
Key point:
- Identity can be sharpened through encounters with external powers or competing communities.
- The chapter rejects reducing Palestinian identity to a reaction to Zionism alone.
6. Q: How does Khalidi distinguish the chapter’s historical argument from later claims that Palestinian nationalism existed unchanged for millennia?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that many elements of attachment to Palestine were old, but the modern national form of that attachment was relatively recent. He cautions against reading a fully developed modern national consciousness back into earlier centuries or treating ancient populations as direct evidence of an unchanged modern identity. The chapter instead traces how older religious, local, administrative, and patriotic attachments were combined in new ways under modern political conditions. This approach recognizes historical continuity without assuming that the meaning of identity remained identical across very different periods.
Study instruction:
- "Separate evidence of long-standing attachment to place from evidence of a modern national political identity."
Key point:
- The chapter recognizes older roots while treating modern nationalism as a historical transformation.
- Avoid anachronistically projecting contemporary national categories onto earlier centuries.
7. Q: What competing Arabist and Islamist perspectives on Palestinian nationalism does the chapter describe?
A: Show answer
Khalidi discusses radical Arab nationalist views that regard modern Arab nation-states as artificial creations imposed by Western imperial powers, and radical Islamist views that regard territorially defined nationalism as a division of the wider Muslim community. Palestinian Islamist organizations have often placed Palestinian politics within a universal Islamic framework, while mainstream secular Palestinian nationalism, represented in the chapter by the PLO and its constituent groups, gives precedence to the specifically Palestinian dimension. Khalidi notes that these positions can coexist with the practical reality that many organizations are rooted in Palestinian membership, institutions, and political goals.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the Arabist, Islamist, and secular Palestinian nationalist positions described in the chapter, noting their different scales of political belonging."
Key point:
- Palestinian identity has been debated within wider Arab and Islamic frameworks.
- Political movements can combine universalist language with specifically Palestinian organization and goals.
8. Q: What is the chapter’s main chronological claim about the years 1917–1923?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the years from the First World War through the early British Mandate were unusually rapid and transformative. By about 1922 or 1923, a significant portion of politically conscious, literate, and urban Palestinians had developed a strong identification with Palestine as a single community. This did not mean that every class or region had changed at the same pace. The initial shift was most visible among educated and politically active groups, and it spread more broadly in later decades through education, the press, urbanization, and wage labor. The chronology therefore distinguishes an early crystallization from later mass diffusion.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a timeline from 1917 to 1923 showing the major political events and the forms of identity that gained or lost importance."
Key point:
- The chapter identifies 1917–1923 as the critical period of rapid identity transformation.
- The early shift was strongest among literate and politically active groups before spreading further.
9. Q: What prewar sources of attachment to Palestine had already developed before the modern national period?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several prewar foundations: religious attachment to the Holy Land among Muslims and Christians; a conception of Palestine as a recognizable geographic and historical entity; Ottoman administrative arrangements centered on Jerusalem; concern about European encroachment; and strong local loyalties to cities, villages, and families. Education, the press, roads, and railways gradually helped people connect these attachments to a larger geographic community. Khalidi’s point is that the postwar national identification built on existing forms of belonging rather than appearing in a social vacuum.
Study instruction:
- "Organize the prewar foundations into religious, administrative, geopolitical, local, and social categories."
Key point:
- Palestinian identity drew on several long-standing forms of attachment to the country.
- Modern national consciousness developed by combining and reinterpreting earlier loyalties.
10. Q: How did Muslim and Christian religious traditions contribute to a shared conception of Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that Muslims and Christians held different religious understandings of why Palestine was sacred and where its boundaries lay, yet both traditions treated the country as a special and meaningful whole. Christian conceptions drew on biblical geography and the jurisdiction of major churches based in Jerusalem. Muslim conceptions were reinforced by Qur’anic references to the Holy Land, devotional literature praising Jerusalem and other sites, and pilgrimage practices such as the Nabi Musa celebration. These traditions did not create an identical political identity, but they helped sustain a shared sense that Palestine was a distinctive geographic and sacred entity.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the Christian and Muslim sources of attachment described in the chapter and identify the common geographic idea they reinforced."
Key point:
- Different religious traditions contributed to a shared conception of Palestine as a special land.
- Religious attachment provided an important pre-national foundation for broader identification.
11. Q: What role did the Christian idea of the biblical land “from Dan to Beersheba” play?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites the biblical definition as one basis for a Christian conception of Palestine as a recognizable whole. This understanding was reinforced by the geographic reach of the Greek Orthodox and Latin Patriarchates and the Protestant Episcopate of Jerusalem, whose jurisdictions crossed changing Ottoman administrative boundaries. Khalidi uses the example to show that a sense of Palestine’s geographic extent could exist even when there was no Ottoman province bearing that exact name throughout the period. Religious institutions therefore helped preserve a conception of the country that was not reducible to the current administrative map.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how religious jurisdictions can create a geographic understanding that differs from the administrative boundaries of a state."
Key point:
- Christian religious geography helped define Palestine as a recognizable territorial whole.
- Religious boundaries could persist across changing political and administrative divisions.
12. Q: How did Muslim devotional literature contribute to the idea of Palestine as a distinct land?
A: Show answer
Khalidi points to the Fada’il al-Quds literature, which praised the merits of Jerusalem, Hebron, and other places in Palestine. This tradition was widespread before the Crusades and expanded afterward. It encouraged Muslims to understand Palestine as a sacred geographic entity, even if the basis of that unity was religious rather than modern political sovereignty. The chapter also mentions local pilgrimage practices, especially the Nabi Musa festival, which drew people from across the country to a shared site. These practices helped connect communities to places beyond their immediate town or village.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the devotional texts and pilgrimage practices mentioned in the chapter and explain how they reinforced a sense of geographic unity."
Key point:
- Muslim sacred literature and pilgrimage helped sustain a shared conception of Palestine.
- Religious geography connected people across local boundaries.
13. Q: Why was the Nabi Musa pilgrimage important to the chapter’s account of Palestinian attachment to place?
A: Show answer
The annual Nabi Musa celebration brought thousands of Muslim pilgrims from different parts of Palestine to a shrine between Jerusalem and Jericho. Khalidi uses it as an example of a recurring practice that connected people across localities and reinforced the idea of the country as a shared sacred space. Such gatherings did not by themselves create modern nationalism, but they provided an established framework for collective movement, memory, and belonging. They also show that local religious practices could operate across a wider geographic area than an individual village or city.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how recurring pilgrimage routes and shared festivals can create connections among communities that otherwise live far apart."
Key point:
- The Nabi Musa celebration linked communities through shared religious practice.
- Common practices helped reinforce a geographic conception of Palestine before modern nationalism.
14. Q: How did Ottoman administrative boundaries contribute to the conception of Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that from 1874 the Jerusalem sanjaq was administered separately from other Ottoman provinces and placed directly under Istanbul. Its districts included Jerusalem, Bethlehem, Hebron, Beersheba, Gaza, and Jaffa. Earlier arrangements had at times included Jerusalem in a larger province called Filastin or placed it within the province of Damascus. These arrangements changed, but the long-standing special status of Jerusalem helped make Palestine recognizable as an administrative and political unit. Administrative practice could therefore strengthen a geographic consciousness even without a stable, continuously existing province with modern borders.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the changing status of the Jerusalem sanjaq and explain why its long period of separate administration mattered."
Key point:
- Ottoman administration helped reinforce the idea of Palestine as a distinct entity.
- Administrative boundaries contributed to identity even when their form changed over time.
15. Q: What did Najib ‘Azuri argue about the Jerusalem sanjaq in 1908?
A: Show answer
Najib ‘Azuri recommended expanding the Jerusalem sanjaq and raising it to the rank of a vilayet because he believed the progress of the land of Palestine depended on such an arrangement. Khalidi uses this proposal to show that an educated political observer could speak of Palestine as a coherent geographic entity before the First World War. ‘Azuri’s opposition to Zionism was one factor behind his argument, but the proposal also assumed that there was a recognizable “land of Palestine” whose administration should be organized accordingly. The example links administrative reform, political concern, and local territorial consciousness.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what the proposal reveals about the relationship between administrative organization and a sense of territorial identity."
Key point:
- ‘Azuri’s proposal treated Palestine as a meaningful geographic and administrative unit.
- The idea of Palestine as a distinct land was already present in prewar political discussion.
16. Q: How did European imperial ambitions help strengthen attachment to Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that repeated European efforts to expand influence in Palestine made inhabitants more aware that control of the country was coveted by outside powers. This sense of external threat could strengthen community feeling and patriotic attachment. The chapter gives examples of Muslim concerns reaching back to the Crusades and to a 1701 petition protesting a French consul’s visit to Jerusalem. In the nineteenth century, many Arab Christians also became concerned about European imperialism, even as missionary schools introduced some of them to modern nationalist ideas. External pressure therefore helped unite people with different religious and social backgrounds around a shared concern for the country.
Study instruction:
- "Identify examples of external intervention and explain how they could encourage a shared local or territorial attachment."
Key point:
- European imperial ambitions contributed to a sense that Palestine was a community under external pressure.
- Concern about foreign encroachment could bridge some religious and social differences.
17. Q: What does the 1701 Jerusalem petition reveal about earlier feelings of collective belonging?
A: Show answer
The petition to Sultan Mustafa II protested a French consul’s visit and described Jerusalem as attracting the attention of foreign powers, warning that the Holy Land might be occupied as had happened in earlier times. Khalidi notes that both notables and ordinary people attended the meeting that produced the petition. This suggests that concern about external control was not confined to a small political elite. The document is used as evidence of an older sense of collective responsibility for the city and the surrounding sacred land, even though the language was religious and local rather than modern nationalist.
Study instruction:
- "Assess what the petition reveals about who participated, what threat they perceived, and the kind of collective identity expressed."
Key point:
- Concern about foreign encroachment could involve both notables and ordinary residents.
- Earlier collective attachment was often expressed in religious and local rather than modern national language.
18. Q: How did local patriotism shape identity in Palestinian cities and villages?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes strong attachments to Jerusalem, Nablus, Gaza, Hebron, and other localities. City histories, family names, and regional identifiers such as al-Maqdisi, al-Nabulsi, and al-Khalili reflected pride in place. Villagers also felt attachment to their communities, lineages, and local landscapes. These loyalties were not simply obstacles to broader identity. As education and travel expanded, local attachments could be supplemented by a sense of belonging to Palestine as a larger whole. They remained meaningful even after a stronger national identification developed.
Study instruction:
- "Give examples of urban and village-based identity markers and explain how they could coexist with a broader Palestinian identification."
Key point:
- Local patriotism was a durable foundation of Palestinian belonging.
- Broader national identity supplemented rather than erased many local loyalties.
19. Q: How did improved travel and education help people develop a sense of belonging beyond their immediate locality?
A: Show answer
The spread of modern education introduced broader geographic and political ideas to more people, while roads and railways made travel easier and faster. These changes allowed people to encounter places and communities beyond their town or village and to understand local concerns as part of a larger territorial space. Khalidi argues that this process supplemented existing local loyalties rather than replacing them. It helped make Palestine as a whole more present in people’s mental maps and created conditions for a wider national identification to develop.
Study instruction:
- "Connect education, transport, and communication to the expansion of geographic awareness beyond a single locality."
Key point:
- Modern education and mobility helped connect local communities to a larger territorial idea.
- New forms of belonging grew alongside older attachments to city, village, and family.
20. Q: Why does Khalidi reject the idea that Palestinian identity was merely a reaction to Zionism?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies religious, administrative, geopolitical, and local foundations of attachment to Palestine that predated modern political Zionism. Zionism did become a major factor because it sharpened concern about the country’s future and encouraged Palestinians to see themselves as part of a larger community facing a common challenge. But it was not the only external “other,” nor did it create the underlying attachment to place from nothing. Khalidi therefore treats Zionism as an accelerant and defining political challenge within a longer history of Palestinian belonging.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the pre-existing sources of attachment from the ways the encounter with Zionism intensified and reshaped them."
Key point:
- Palestinian attachment to the country predated modern political Zionism.
- Zionism accelerated the crystallization of identity but did not create every element of it.
21. Q: What did the 1914 Filastin editorial mean by referring to a “Palestinian nation”?
A: Show answer
In an open letter to subscribers, the editors of Filastin described their newspaper as warning the “Palestinian nation” of the danger posed by the Zionist movement. They repeated that the nation was threatened with disappearance in its own land and defended the paper’s patriotic duty despite an Ottoman attempt to close it. Khalidi regards this terminology as significant because it shows a clearly articulated Palestinian national reference before the First World War. The editorial also distinguished Palestinian patriotism from loyalty to the Ottoman state and from broader Arab identity, while allowing these loyalties to coexist.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze the editorial’s use of the terms nation, Palestine, Arab, and Ottoman, and explain what each contributes to its argument."
Key point:
- The 1914 editorial provides direct evidence of prewar use of Palestinian national language.
- Palestinian patriotism was articulated alongside other forms of political loyalty.
22. Q: Why was the court victory by Filastin’s editors in 1914 politically significant?
A: Show answer
The Ottoman authorities attempted to close Filastin because of its criticism of Zionism, but editors Yusuf and ‘Isa al-‘Isa challenged the order in a local court and won. Contemporary French consular reports described a crowd carrying them from the courtroom in celebration. Khalidi uses the episode to show that the newspaper’s defense of Palestine resonated beyond its editorial office and had visible public support. It also demonstrates that the press was a site of political struggle in which questions of censorship, public opinion, and the legitimacy of Palestinian national language became intertwined.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a legal dispute over newspaper publication could become a public demonstration of political identity."
Key point:
- The court case showed that press freedom and Palestinian patriotism had a public constituency.
- The episode made the language of Palestinian nationhood visible before the war.
23. Q: How did ‘Isa al-‘Isa describe the multiple loyalties that motivated his work with Filastin?
A: Show answer
In a later speech, ‘Isa al-‘Isa recalled that his original aim in founding Filastin was to defend the Arab Orthodox cause. He soon found himself involved in two wider national conflicts, one Arab-Turkish and the other Arab-Jewish, without abandoning his Orthodox commitment. Khalidi uses the account to demonstrate that religious, Arab, and Palestinian political attachments could be compatible for an individual. The example challenges the assumption that a person must choose one exclusive identity and illustrates how a local cause could expand into a broader political role.
Study instruction:
- "List the different causes mentioned by al-‘Isa and explain how they could coexist in one person’s political life."
Key point:
- Al-‘Isa’s account illustrates the compatibility of religious, Arab, and Palestinian loyalties.
- Political identity could expand without requiring the abandonment of earlier commitments.
24. Q: How did the First World War weaken Ottomanism as a source of identity?
A: Show answer
The defeat of the Ottoman army and withdrawal of Ottoman authority from the Arab provinces by the end of 1918 removed the political framework within which generations had lived. The war also undermined Ottomanism as an ideological synthesis across different peoples and communities. National self-determination gained prestige, especially after President Wilson’s Fourteen Points, while Turkish nationalism and CUP policies had already weakened the appeal of Ottoman unity for many Arabs. The chapter notes that Ottoman legacies continued to matter, but Ottomanism as a primary political identity rapidly lost force in Palestine and other Arab provinces.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the military, political, and ideological developments that reduced the appeal of Ottomanism between 1914 and 1918."
Key point:
- The collapse of Ottoman authority created a major political and psychological vacuum.
- Nationalism gained ground as the Ottoman imperial framework disintegrated.
25. Q: Did the decline of Ottomanism mean that religion ceased to matter as a source of identity?
A: Show answer
No. Khalidi argues that religious affiliation also lost some of its earlier political centrality as the Ottoman state’s religious legitimacy weakened and the CUP was seen by some as exploiting Islam while pursuing a secular Turkish nationalist project. This contributed to a shift among parts of the younger elite toward secular nationalism. But the chapter explicitly cautions that religion remained extremely important for many people, especially rural populations, lower classes, and older urban residents. The decline of religion as the governing principle of the state should therefore not be confused with the disappearance of religious belonging from everyday life.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the weakening of religion as an imperial political principle from the continuing importance of personal and communal religious identity."
Key point:
- Religious identity remained significant even as Ottomanism and state-centered religious authority weakened.
- Changes among younger elites should not be generalized to the entire population.
26. Q: Why did Arab nationalism initially appear to be the likely successor to Ottomanism after the war?
A: Show answer
With the Ottoman Empire gone, Arabism seemed to offer a political framework capable of uniting the former Arab provinces. The prospect of a federation or connected Arab states, supported by Hashemite leadership and the prestige of the new government in Damascus, gave this idea considerable appeal. Many Palestinians initially viewed the Arab state under Amir Faysal as the realization of wider Arab independence and unity. However, the chapter shows that the political reality in Palestine was more complicated because British policy, the Balfour Declaration, and the fate of the Syrian state soon undermined this expectation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why Arab nationalism appeared attractive after the Ottoman collapse and identify the events that challenged its practical realization in Palestine."
Key point:
- Arabism initially appeared to fill the political vacuum left by Ottomanism.
- The failure of wider Arab political projects helped redirect Palestinian political focus.
27. Q: What material effects did the First World War have on Palestine?
A: Show answer
The war brought military campaigns, blockades, requisitions, famine, destruction, forced conscription, executions, and exile. Ottoman forces used Palestine as a base for campaigns toward the Suez Canal, while British forces advanced north through the country in 1917 and 1918. Trees were cut for fuel, draft animals were taken by both armies, agricultural and trade networks were disrupted, and many draft-age men never returned from military service. Khalidi cites demographic research estimating that Palestine’s population fell by more than six percent in a little over four years, a sharp reversal after prewar annual growth of about two percent.
Study instruction:
- "Group the war’s effects into military, economic, demographic, and social consequences, and identify one example in each category."
Key point:
- The war severely disrupted Palestine’s economy, population, and social structure.
- Material devastation formed part of the context for rapid changes in political identity.
28. Q: Why were the political and psychological consequences of the war even more important than its material effects?
A: Show answer
The collapse of the Ottoman state removed a familiar political framework and created uncertainty about who would govern Palestine. British and French occupation, long feared by many inhabitants, became a reality. The Balfour Declaration and the disclosure of the Sykes-Picot agreement showed that outside powers had made decisions about the region without the consent of its population. These developments forced Palestinians to reassess their political loyalties and the institutions that might protect their interests. Khalidi argues that the speed and scale of these changes accelerated a transformation in collective self-understanding that had been developing more slowly before the war.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the loss of a familiar state and the arrival of new foreign authorities could change political identity more quickly than ordinary social change."
Key point:
- The war created a political vacuum and a new set of external threats.
- Crisis accelerated changes in collective self-understanding.
29. Q: What were the three major British wartime engagements concerning the Arab lands and Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies the Husayn-McMahon correspondence of 1915–1916, the Sykes-Picot agreement of 1916, and the Balfour Declaration of 1917. In the correspondence, Britain promised to support Arab independence within boundaries that remained disputed. Sykes-Picot divided Ottoman Arab provinces into British and French spheres and zones of control. The Balfour Declaration promised British support for a national home for the Jewish people in Palestine. Only the Balfour Declaration was publicly issued at the time; the Sykes-Picot agreement was revealed by the Bolsheviks after the Russian Revolution. Together, the engagements created conflicting expectations and commitments.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison table showing the date, parties, stated purpose, and implications of each of the three engagements."
Key point:
- Britain made multiple commitments whose implications for Palestine were conflicting and disputed.
- The three engagements became central to postwar uncertainty and political grievance.
30. Q: Why was the question of whether Palestine was included in the Husayn-McMahon correspondence so contentious?
A: Show answer
The British letters promised support for Arab independence within boundaries that were not precisely or consistently defined. Whether Palestine fell within the promised area became one of the most disputed issues of the interwar period and later scholarship. Khalidi notes that the question was ultimately addressed at an international conference in London in 1939, showing how long the ambiguity remained politically significant. The chapter uses the correspondence to illustrate the uncertainty created by wartime diplomacy and the gap between Arab expectations and the later policies of Britain and France.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how ambiguous boundaries in diplomatic correspondence can create long-term disputes about the meaning of a promise."
Key point:
- The scope of the British promise to Sharif Husayn was disputed, especially regarding Palestine.
- Ambiguity in wartime agreements shaped postwar political grievances.
31. Q: How did the Balfour Declaration affect Palestinian perceptions after 1917?
A: Show answer
The declaration publicly committed Britain to supporting a national home for the Jewish people in Palestine. Khalidi argues that its impact was intensified because it appeared at the moment when British forces were occupying the country and the Sykes-Picot agreement had just become known. Palestinians who had feared Zionist political objectives now faced a major imperial power publicly supporting them. The declaration therefore transformed a long-standing concern into a direct question about the future government and political status of the country. It also weakened confidence that Britain would protect the interests of the existing Arab population.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the timing of the Balfour Declaration, the British military occupation, and the disclosure of Sykes-Picot mattered together."
Key point:
- The Balfour Declaration made British support for Zionist aims an immediate political fact.
- It intensified concerns that Palestinians would not control their own political future.
32. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize the rapidity of identity change during periods of crisis?
A: Show answer
Khalidi observes that social attitudes often evolve slowly during periods of relative stability but can change rapidly when people face major shocks to their political and economic world. The war, imperial occupation, and collapse of expected political arrangements forced Palestinians to reconsider basic assumptions about authority and community. Yet crisis alone does not guarantee a new identity; people also need a viable vision or alternative around which to organize. In Palestine, the combination of existing attachments, emerging nationalist ideas, and a perceived common threat helped produce a rapid shift toward stronger identification with the country.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why crisis can accelerate identity change, and identify the alternative political vision that made change possible in the Palestinian case."
Key point:
- Crisis can accelerate changes that would otherwise develop more gradually.
- A shared alternative or political goal is needed for crisis to produce a coherent new identity.
33. Q: Why was it difficult to reconstruct Palestinian attitudes during the war and the first postwar year?
A: Show answer
The local press was shut down at the outbreak of war and did not return until 1919, while military rule, censorship, famine, and political repression limited public expression. Researchers are therefore left with memoirs, private papers, a limited number of documents, and occasional pamphlets or interviews published outside Palestine. British and Zionist sources are more abundant, but Khalidi warns that many officials lacked knowledge of Palestinian society and were shaped by prejudice, limiting their value for reconstructing local attitudes. The chapter’s argument must therefore be built from an incomplete and uneven record.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the main source types available for the war years and explain the limitations of official British and Zionist observations."
Key point:
- The wartime evidence for Palestinian attitudes is fragmentary and uneven.
- Abundant official records do not automatically provide reliable access to local perceptions.
34. Q: How did British censorship affect the revival of the Palestinian press after the war?
A: Show answer
The local press gradually reappeared from 1919, initially under British military censorship. The country remained under the Occupied Enemy Territory Administration until 1920, and a civilian administration continued to control publication afterward. The chapter cites examples of officials refusing permission to publish a newspaper or open a printing press and summoning nationalist editors for questioning. These restrictions delayed the return of major prewar papers and limited what journalists could say. Despite this, the press remained crucial for understanding how Palestinians interpreted the new political situation and articulated their identity.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the return of the press from 1919 and identify the mechanisms through which British authorities controlled publication."
Key point:
- The postwar press operated under military and then civilian censorship.
- Restrictions shaped both the content and the timing of public political debate.
35. Q: Why did Filastin and al-Karmil resume publication at different times after the war?
A: Show answer
Filastin did not resume publication until March 1921, in part because ‘Isa al-‘Isa had been exiled by the Ottoman authorities, served in Faysal’s government in Damascus, and faced what appears to have been a British restriction on reentry. Al-Karmil resumed in Haifa in February 1920, while al-Nafir reappeared earlier, in September 1919. These different dates reflect the effects of wartime exile, deaths, institutional disruption, censorship, and the difficulty of rebuilding newspapers in a society damaged by war and famine. Publication histories therefore reveal the political and material conditions under which identity was debated.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the circumstances that delayed the return of different newspapers and explain how personal exile and censorship affected public communication."
Key point:
- The revival of the press was uneven and shaped by war, exile, and government restrictions.
- The timing of a newspaper’s return is part of the history of political expression.
36. Q: What was Suriyya al-Janubiyya, and why was it important in the immediate postwar period?
A: Show answer
Suriyya al-Janubiyya, or Southern Syria, began publication in Jerusalem in September 1919 under Muhammad Hassan al-Budayri and was edited by ‘Arif al-‘Arif. It was highly political, strongly nationalist, and influential during its short life. Affiliated with the Arab nationalist club al-Nadi al-‘Arabi, it attracted prominent writers and reflected the prestige of the Arab state established in Damascus under Amir Faysal. The British authorities took the paper seriously, closing it temporarily after its first ten issues and permanently after the disturbances of April 1920. Its name and slogans reveal the initial appeal of a united Syrian and Arab political future.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the paper’s editors, political affiliation, slogans, and the events that led to its closure."
Key point:
- Suriyya al-Janubiyya was a major early postwar platform for Arab nationalist opinion in Palestine.
- Its short life reveals the initial appeal and later weakening of the Southern Syrian idea.
37. Q: What did the title “Southern Syria” signify for many Palestinians in 1919?
A: Show answer
The title reflected the hope that Palestine would remain part of a united greater Syria under Faysal’s Arab government in Damascus, as a first stage toward broader Arab unity. For many Palestinians, this was not simply a statement that Palestine lacked its own identity. It expressed commitment to Arabism and to a political project that seemed capable of protecting the region from European partition and Zionist settlement. The idea was initially powerful but depended on the survival of the Syrian state and on political leaders being willing and able to defend Palestinian interests within it.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the Southern Syrian idea was attractive in 1919 and what political assumptions it depended upon."
Key point:
- Southern Syria expressed a postwar hope for Arab unity centered on Damascus.
- The idea was a political orientation that could coexist with a growing Palestinian focus.
38. Q: Why did the Arab state under Faysal matter so much to Palestinians after the war?
A: Show answer
For many Arab nationalists, Faysal’s government in Damascus appeared to be the first modern Arab state and a possible foundation for wider independence and unity. Palestinians served in its institutions, sat in its parliament through surviving Ottoman deputies, and identified with its aspirations. The state claimed authority over the Syrian coast and Palestine, even though Britain and France did not recognize its sovereignty over those areas. The project therefore offered hope but also exposed Palestinians to decisions made by Syrian leaders whose priorities did not always match Palestinian concerns, especially regarding Zionism.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the political and symbolic importance of Faysal’s state and distinguish its claims from the authority actually exercised by Britain and France."
Key point:
- Faysal’s state embodied a major Arab nationalist hope after the Ottoman collapse.
- Its limited authority and competing interests created uncertainty for Palestinians.
39. Q: Why did the French destruction of Faysal’s Syrian state in July 1920 change Palestinian political thinking?
A: Show answer
The French conquest of Damascus and removal of Faysal ended the political project that many Palestinians had hoped would protect Arab unity and independence. The loss of the Syrian state forced Palestinian leaders to reconsider their plans, especially because British and French control had already limited the country’s political options. Musa Kazim al-Husayni summarized the shift by saying that Southern Syria no longer existed and Palestine now had to be defended. Khalidi treats the event as a major turning point in the movement from a wider Syrian focus toward a more specifically Palestinian political identity.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the consequences of the fall of Faysal’s state for Palestinian political strategy and the language used by nationalist leaders."
Key point:
- The fall of Damascus removed a central political focus for Palestinian Arabists.
- The loss accelerated the shift toward defending Palestine as a distinct political entity.
40. Q: How did the language of Suriyya al-Janubiyya change as the postwar situation developed?
A: Show answer
Early issues emphasized Arab unity, Syrian independence, and the hope that Palestine would remain part of a wider Syrian state. The newspaper’s slogans referred to the Arab nation and independence, and its reporting focused heavily on the Paris peace conference and the possibility of partition. Over time, however, it also published more explicitly Palestinian expressions of loyalty and national feeling. This change did not immediately replace Arabism, but it showed that a specifically Palestinian focus was gaining importance even within a newspaper whose title and political orientation were strongly Southern Syrian.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the paper’s early masthead slogans with its later articles devoted specifically to Palestine."
Key point:
- The newspaper’s language reveals a gradual shift from a primarily Syrian frame toward a stronger Palestinian one.
- Arabism and Palestinian patriotism overlapped during the transition.
41. Q: What was the significance of the article “Manajat Filastin” published in January 1920?
A: Show answer
The article, signed “Ibn al-Jazira,” addressed Palestine directly in an intensely patriotic and emotional style. It described the country as the homeland of the writer and ancestors and celebrated its historical and religious associations. Khalidi regards it as an example of fully developed nationalist rhetoric focused solely on Palestine rather than on the whole Arab region. Its appearance in Suriyya al-Janubiyya is especially significant because the newspaper was otherwise strongly Arabist and committed to the idea of greater Syria. The article therefore signals a shift in the direction of Palestinian-specific identification.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the language that makes the article specifically Palestinian and explain why its publication in an Arabist newspaper matters."
Key point:
- The article offered an explicit expression of loyalty to Palestine as a distinct homeland.
- Its appearance shows Palestinian patriotism growing within a wider Arab nationalist setting.
42. Q: How did the Muslim-Christian Society in Gaza illustrate the development of Palestinian patriotism?
A: Show answer
A March 1920 report described the creation of a Muslim-Christian Society in Gaza intended to build a united front against Zionism and attempts by the British and Zionists to divide the Arab population along religious lines. The article celebrated the removal of older religious sensitivities and described the organization as serving Palestinian patriotism in particular and Arab patriotism in general. Khalidi sees this wording as important because it gives Palestinian and Arab patriotism distinct but compatible meanings, with the specifically Palestinian dimension becoming central to practical political activity.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a cross-religious civic organization could express both local national solidarity and wider Arab affiliation."
Key point:
- The Gaza society connected Muslim-Christian cooperation with Palestinian political organization.
- The distinction between Palestinian and Arab patriotism was becoming more explicit.
43. Q: What does the phrase “Palestinian patriotism in particular and Arab patriotism in general” reveal?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the phrase placed Palestinian patriotism and Arab patriotism side by side but gave the Palestinian dimension priority in practical political work. People in Palestine faced specific problems under British rule and within the boundaries imposed by the new authorities, so their immediate political activity increasingly centered on their own country. Commitment to the wider Arab cause continued, but it could no longer substitute for a concrete program to defend Palestine. The phrase therefore captures the transition from overlapping loyalties toward a more territorially focused Palestinian nationalism.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the order and wording of the phrase matter for understanding the relationship between local and regional identity."
Key point:
- The phrase distinguishes Palestinian and Arab patriotism while preserving both.
- Practical political needs increasingly privileged the Palestinian focus.
44. Q: Why does the chapter treat the term “the capital of Palestine” in al-Sabah as significant?
A: Show answer
Suriyya al-Janubiyya had referred to Damascus as “the capital” while Faysal’s government existed there. After the fall of the Syrian state, al-Sabah’s first issue in October 1921 described Jerusalem as “the capital of Palestine.” Khalidi treats the small wording change as evidence of a substantial shift in political focus within a short period. Jerusalem was now being identified as the center of a distinct Palestinian political space rather than as a locality within a wider Syrian state. Such everyday language can reveal changes in identity that are not always announced through formal declarations.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the newspaper’s use of “the capital” in 1920 with “the capital of Palestine” in 1921 and explain the implied political shift."
Key point:
- The phrase marked Jerusalem as the center of a distinct Palestinian political entity.
- Small changes in newspaper terminology can reveal important changes in political imagination.
45. Q: How did Mir’at al-Sharq express a Palestinian focus without strongly emphasizing Arabism?
A: Show answer
The newspaper’s first editorial described it as being published in Jerusalem “among our people” without explicitly defining the nation as Arab or Palestinian. Khalidi interprets this wording as implying a Palestinian focus while leaving the term umma open to more than one meaning. The paper was treated more favorably by British officials than its more militant nationalist rival, Suriyya al-Janubiyya, and was used by the Chief Administrator to respond to nationalist agitation. Its example shows that Palestinian-oriented discourse could appear in different editorial styles and under different political circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze the ambiguity of the word umma and explain how local context can indicate the intended community even when the wording remains open."
Key point:
- Mir’at al-Sharq reflected a Palestinian focus without using the same explicit Arabist language as its rival.
- Newspaper identity and political orientation must be read through wording and context together.
46. Q: How did British policy contribute to the decline of the Southern Syrian idea among Palestinians?
A: Show answer
Britain did not allow Faysal’s state to extend its authority into Palestine and ultimately accepted French control over Syria. The British also supported the Balfour Declaration and maintained a separate military and then civilian administration in Palestine. These policies made the wider Syrian project less capable of protecting Palestinian interests. As Palestinians confronted their own specific problems under British rule, they increasingly had to organize politically within Palestine. Khalidi presents the shift not as a purely ideological choice but as a response to the collapse of practical political alternatives and the imposition of new territorial boundaries.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the British decisions that limited the Southern Syrian project and explain how they redirected Palestinian political activity."
Key point:
- British policy prevented the Syrian state from exercising authority in Palestine.
- The failure of the wider political project encouraged more Palestine-centered organization.
47. Q: Why did some Palestinians become disillusioned with Faysal and Syrian Arab leaders?
A: Show answer
Khalidi explains that some Palestinians, including committed Arabists serving in Damascus, became concerned that Syrian leaders might compromise on Palestine in order to secure an independent Syrian state against French pressure. Faysal’s agreement with Chaim Weizmann and the limited attention some Syrian politicians gave to Zionism deepened this concern. Palestinians feared that their country might be sacrificed in a bargain with Britain or the Zionists. The chapter treats this as a major reason why the Southern Syrian idea lost credibility as a practical vehicle for defending Palestine, even among people who remained committed to Arab unity.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the conflict between the goal of preserving an independent Syria and the goal of protecting Palestinian political rights."
Key point:
- Some Palestinian Arabists believed Syrian leaders were prepared to compromise Palestinian interests.
- The perceived failure of Arab leadership weakened confidence in wider political protection.
48. Q: How did the Balfour Declaration and British occupation affect the reception of Allenby’s entry into Jerusalem?
A: Show answer
Khalidi notes that during Allenby’s ceremonial entry in December 1917, Arab military forces, flags, and representatives were deliberately excluded, unlike the prominent role Arab forces had in some other parts of Syria. This exclusion occurred in a context where Britain had publicly supported a Jewish national home in Palestine. For Palestinians, the ceremony therefore symbolized not simply a change of military authority but also the marginalization of Arab claims in the country. It reinforced the impression that decisions about Palestine were being made by foreign powers without the participation of its Arab population.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the symbolism of a military ceremony can matter to political identity and perceptions of inclusion or exclusion."
Key point:
- The ceremony reinforced Palestinian concerns about exclusion from the new political order.
- British conduct made the shift from Ottoman to British authority politically consequential.
49. Q: Why did the recognition of Hebrew as an official language disturb Palestinians after the British occupation?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the recognition of Hebrew as an official language as an important change made under the British administration despite the proclaimed principle of maintaining the prewar status quo. Language is closely tied to national identity and political authority, so the change was interpreted as a sign that the Zionist movement was gaining official standing. It reinforced fears that the British intended to facilitate a future in which the Jewish community would exercise political dominance. The issue therefore had significance beyond administrative convenience; it was read as a signal about the direction of the new government.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the official status of a language can symbolize political recognition, power, and the future structure of a state."
Key point:
- Language policy became a visible indicator of the changing political order.
- The decision deepened fears about British support for Zionist political objectives.
50. Q: How did the speech by Sir Herbert Samuel intensify Palestinian concern about the future of the country?
A: Show answer
Khalidi discusses Samuel’s statement that the Zionist movement aimed to establish a self-governing commonwealth under the auspices of a Jewish majority. Even though the speech was presented in moderate language, Palestinians interpreted it as confirming their fear that they would eventually become a minority in their own country. The press responded by asserting that Palestine was Arab and must remain Arab. The episode shows how public declarations by British officials could be judged against earlier assurances and against the political consequences Palestinians believed those declarations implied.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the wording of Samuel’s statement with the response in Suriyya al-Janubiyya and explain why the apparent moderation did not reassure readers."
Key point:
- Samuel’s speech reinforced fears of a future Jewish majority and political control.
- Palestinian responses linked demographic change with sovereignty and national belonging.
51. Q: Why did Palestinians increasingly feel politically isolated by the early 1920s?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Palestinians came to feel alone after the Ottoman Empire collapsed, the Syrian state was destroyed by France, and Arab leaders proved unable or unwilling to defend Palestinian interests against British policy and Zionist settlement. Britain controlled Palestine and supported the Balfour Declaration, while the wider Arab projects that had initially appeared promising failed to provide practical protection. This sense of abandonment helped accelerate a more specifically Palestinian response. The chapter presents identity formation as a political adaptation to a situation in which broader affiliations remained meaningful but could no longer solve the immediate problems facing the country.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the different political frameworks that failed to provide effective protection and explain how their failure shaped local organization."
Key point:
- The collapse of Ottoman and Syrian political frameworks increased the sense of Palestinian isolation.
- A more specifically Palestinian political focus emerged in response to unmet local needs.
52. Q: How did the Palestinian delegation to London in 1921 express the changing focus of political identity?
A: Show answer
The delegation represented a coalition of Palestinian political forces seeking to present opposition to the Balfour Declaration and British policy directly to British decision-makers. Al-Sabah described its purpose as serving the Fourth Palestinian Arab Congress and supporting the delegation defending the Palestinian Arab cause in Europe as part of the general Arab cause. Khalidi interprets the wording as showing that Palestine had become the practical focus, while the wider Arab cause remained an acknowledged but more general framework. The delegation therefore illustrates the emergence of organized Palestinian political representation.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze the delegation’s stated purpose and distinguish the specifically Palestinian objective from the broader Arab framing."
Key point:
- The London delegation represented a more organized Palestinian political response.
- Its language placed the Palestinian Arab cause at the center of practical advocacy.
53. Q: What did Filastin mean when it referred to “Palestine and its sister Syria” in 1921?
A: Show answer
In its first issue after the wartime hiatus, Filastin described Palestine and Syria as sister countries. Khalidi treats this wording as evidence that the paper now recognized them as separate political entities within a wider Arab family. The expression preserved a sense of regional kinship while abandoning the idea that Palestine was simply Southern Syria within one unified state. It reflects the emerging discourse of Arab nation-state nationalism, in which independent countries could be described as brothers and sisters even while belonging to a broader Arab community.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the metaphor of sister countries preserves regional solidarity while recognizing separate political entities."
Key point:
- The phrase marked a clear distinction between Palestine and Syria.
- Separate state identity could coexist with a wider Arab familial metaphor.
54. Q: How did the educational system help spread Palestinian national consciousness beyond the urban elite?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that education provided a channel through which the political ideas developed among literate urban groups could reach wider sectors of society. As more children attended government and private schools, they encountered geographic, historical, and civic concepts that presented Palestine as a distinct unit. The growth of schooling also helped politicize parts of the countryside. Although access remained uneven, the educational system gradually carried Palestinian-specific ideas beyond the upper and middle classes in the cities. Education therefore complemented the press and other forms of communication in spreading national consciousness.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how schools can transmit political ideas to groups that do not participate directly in newspapers or formal political organizations."
Key point:
- Education helped extend Palestinian national ideas beyond the early urban political elite.
- Schooling worked alongside the press as a channel of wider identity formation.
55. Q: What do the chapter’s school enrollment figures show about the spread of education in Mandatory Palestine?
A: Show answer
Khalidi reports that just over 20 percent of Arab school-age children were enrolled in 1922–23. By 1947, about 147,000 of an estimated 330,000 Arab school-age population, or 44.5 percent, attended government or private schools. The distribution was uneven: in towns in 1945–46, around 85 percent of boys and 65 percent of girls were in school, while in the countryside the figures were about 65 percent of boys and 10 percent of girls. Only 432 of roughly 800 villages had schools. The data show substantial expansion but also persistent gender and rural-urban inequalities.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the change in overall enrollment and compare the differences between town and countryside and between boys and girls."
Key point:
- School enrollment expanded significantly during the Mandate period but remained uneven.
- Rural and gender inequalities limited the reach of formal education.
56. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize the 1923 geography textbook by Sabri Sharif ‘Abd al-Hadi?
A: Show answer
The textbook, Jughrafiyyat Suriyya wa Filastin al-Tabi‘iyya, discussed the natural geography, agriculture, communications, population, and administrative divisions of Syria and Palestine. Khalidi considers it important because it treated Palestine as a distinct unit requiring separate geographic study. Students across the country could therefore learn to understand Palestine as a separate entity even when British authorities closely monitored the teaching of history to prevent nationalist ideas from spreading. The textbook illustrates how national consciousness could be transmitted through subjects that appeared technical or descriptive rather than overtly political.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a geography textbook can contribute to national identity even when it avoids explicit political argument."
Key point:
- The 1923 textbook treated Palestine as a distinct geographic entity.
- Educational materials could transmit national concepts even under political censorship.
57. Q: How did British control of history teaching differ from the role of geography education in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The British closely monitored the teaching of history because it could become a direct vehicle for nationalist narratives and political claims. Geography, however, could still teach students that Palestine was a distinct territorial unit through its treatment of physical features, agriculture, communications, population, and administration. Khalidi uses the 1923 geography textbook to show that national consciousness could be reinforced indirectly, even when explicit historical instruction was constrained. The distinction demonstrates that identity formation does not depend on one school subject or a formal national curriculum alone.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the political sensitivity of history teaching with the apparently descriptive role of geography in shaping territorial awareness."
Key point:
- Geography could reinforce a sense of Palestine as a separate unit despite restrictions on nationalist history teaching.
- National ideas can be transmitted through ordinary educational content.
58. Q: What limitations does Khalidi acknowledge about the early evidence for Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that the clearest evidence comes from newspapers, political organizations, memoirs, private papers, and the statements of literate and politically active people. These sources reveal a strong shift among important sectors of society but do not establish that every class, village, or individual adopted the same identity at the same time. The press was censored and unavailable during much of the war, while British and Zionist observers often misunderstood Palestinian society. Khalidi therefore distinguishes the emergence of a well-developed identity among visible groups from its later diffusion to broader sectors of the population.
Study instruction:
- "Separate what can be concluded about politically active urban groups from what remains uncertain about less documented rural and lower-class populations."
Key point:
- The evidence is strongest for literate and politically active sectors in the early 1920s.
- The chapter does not assume uniform adoption of national identity across all groups.
59. Q: How does the chapter explain the continuing importance of local loyalties after Palestinian nationalism developed?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that local attachments to cities, villages, families, and lineages were never completely superseded by a broader Palestinian identification. They remained meaningful even among people living away from their places of origin, including later generations of refugees who continued to identify with ancestral towns and villages. National identity therefore added a larger frame of belonging without erasing smaller ones. This layered pattern is important for understanding Palestinian political and social life, where local associations and national affiliation can reinforce one another rather than compete in every context.
Study instruction:
- "Give an example of a local identity that can persist alongside national identity and explain why displacement does not necessarily erase it."
Key point:
- Local loyalties remained powerful after the emergence of Palestinian national consciousness.
- National belonging supplemented rather than replaced many place-based identities.
60. Q: Why does Khalidi describe Palestinian identity as both continuous with the past and newly formed?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies long-standing religious, administrative, local, and patriotic attachments that provided continuity with earlier generations. It also argues that the modern national form of Palestinian identity emerged through a new combination of those elements under the pressures of war, imperial occupation, and political mobilization. The identity was therefore neither an entirely new invention without roots nor an unchanged ancient consciousness. It was a historical synthesis whose relative elements shifted as political conditions changed. This approach allows the chapter to recognize both continuity and transformation without collapsing one into the other.
Study instruction:
- "Write a two-part explanation identifying the older foundations of belonging and the new political conditions that combined them into a modern national identity."
Key point:
- Palestinian identity combined older attachments with new political forms.
- Historical continuity and modern transformation are both necessary to explain its development.
61. Q: What is the chapter’s overall conclusion about the emergence of Palestinian identity by 1922–1923?
A: Show answer
Khalidi concludes that before the British Mandate was formally confirmed by the League of Nations in July 1922, important sectors of the Arab population had already come to identify primarily with Palestine. This identity remained strongly connected with Arabism and religious sentiment, and it coexisted with local attachments. It was initially most visible among urban, literate, and politically active groups, but it spread in later years through newspapers, education, urbanization, and social change. By the early 1920s, the basic form of Palestinian self-identification had emerged, even though its reach and expression continued to develop.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter’s conclusion in terms of chronology, social reach, and the relationship between Palestinian, Arab, religious, and local affiliations."
Key point:
- A distinct Palestinian national identification had emerged among important sectors by the early 1920s.
- The identity remained layered and continued to spread and develop during the Mandate period.
Rows: 61
Table 8
The Disappearance and Reemergence of Palestinian Identity: Nakba, Pan-Arabism, Political Failure, and National Survival
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 8, “The Disappearance and Reemergence of Palestinian Identity”?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the apparent disappearance of Palestinians from the political map after 1948 should not be confused with the disappearance of Palestinian identity. The Nakba fragmented Palestinian society and weakened visible independent political organization, while pan-Arabism sometimes obscured the distinct Palestinian focus. Yet identity persisted and was rebuilt through refugees, students, clandestine organizations, and later the PLO. The chapter also examines how repeated political and military defeats shaped a narrative of steadfastness and survival. Its central conclusion is that Palestinian identity survived without a sovereign state and eventually reemerged as an internationally recognized political claim.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the loss of territory, institutions, and visible political organization from the persistence of a people’s shared identity.
Key point:
- A temporary decline in visible political expression does not prove that a national identity ceased to exist.
- Palestinian identity persisted through fragmentation and reorganization.
2. Q: What three broad periods does the chapter identify in the modern history of Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the British Mandate and the struggle against British rule and Zionism; the Nakba of 1947–1949, which displaced a large part of the population and destroyed the prospect of an Arab state in Palestine; and the “lost years” from 1948 until the emergence of the PLO in 1964, when Palestinians appeared to lack an independent political center. These periods are connected rather than separate stories. The Mandate shaped the national movement, the Nakba devastated its institutions and social base, and the post-1948 period generated new forms of organization that eventually reconstituted Palestinian political agency.
Study instruction:
- Build a timeline from the Mandate through 1964 and identify the main institutional and social change in each period.
Key point:
- The “disappearance” narrative is linked to the post-1948 hiatus in visible political organization.
- The three periods form a connected process of national struggle, defeat, and reorganization.
3. Q: Why is it misleading to claim that Palestinian identity first emerged in the 1960s?
A: Show answer
The claim confuses a period when Palestinian political activity was less visible to outside observers with the origin of the identity itself. Earlier chapters of Khalidi’s study trace the development of Palestinian identification from the late Ottoman period through the Mandate. After 1948, displacement, citizenship in different states, military rule, and the absence of a central political institution made the identity harder to observe. The later emergence of the PLO and independent organizations made Palestinian agency more visible, but they built on earlier political language, symbols, and experiences rather than creating an entirely new people in the 1960s.
Study instruction:
- Separate the chronology of identity formation from the chronology of the institutions that publicly represented it.
Key point:
- The 1960s marked a new phase of visible political organization, not the beginning of Palestinian identity.
- Historical continuity can persist through periods of institutional weakness.
4. Q: Why does the chapter give special importance to the fighting inside Palestine before May 15, 1948?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that the phase from November 1947 to mid-May 1948 was decisive because Palestinian forces suffered major defeats before the entry of the Arab state armies. Zionist military organizations, including the Haganah and Palmach, defeated a disorganized Palestinian population and captured major cities such as Jaffa and Haifa, as well as many towns, villages, and large areas of land. This phase destroyed much of the Palestinian social and economic base and made the proposed Arab state in the UN partition plan increasingly unattainable. It is often overshadowed by the subsequent interstate Arab-Israeli war, but the chapter regards it as foundational to the Nakba.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the pre-May 15 fighting from the later interstate war and list the consequences of each phase.
Key point:
- The decisive collapse of Palestinian society began before the entry of the Arab armies.
- The loss of major urban centers and rural communities transformed the country’s demographic and political structure.
5. Q: What role did UN General Assembly Resolution 181 play in the events of 1947–1948?
A: Show answer
Resolution 181, adopted on November 29, 1947, proposed partitioning Palestine into an Arab state and a Jewish state. Khalidi argues that the Arab state envisaged by the resolution was effectively prevented from coming into existence by the military defeat of the Palestinians, the establishment of Israel, and the actions of regional leaders. The chapter uses the phrase that the Arab state was “strangled at birth” to emphasize that the political outcome was not simply a failure to implement a neutral plan; it followed a decisive change in control on the ground. The resolution therefore forms part of the context for the destruction of Palestinian political hopes in 1948.
Study instruction:
- Explain the partition proposal and distinguish the proposed political arrangement from the outcome produced by the 1947–1949 fighting.
Key point:
- Resolution 181 proposed two states, but the Arab state did not materialize.
- Military outcomes and regional politics determined the post-war territorial reality.
6. Q: What does the chapter say about the scale and duration of Palestinian displacement in 1947–1949?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes the flight and expulsion of roughly half of Palestine’s Arab population, estimated in the chapter at about 1.4 million before the process. Displacement began before May 15, 1948 and continued through the fighting and armistice arrangements of 1949. The chapter stresses that precise refugee numbers remain disputed and difficult to establish. It also notes the depopulation of more than four hundred cities, towns, and villages, whose inhabitants were dispersed across the region. The result was not only a refugee crisis but a transformation of the population, economy, social networks, and geography of Palestine.
Study instruction:
- Identify which figures are estimates and explain why refugee totals and the chronology of displacement require careful source criticism.
Key point:
- Displacement was a process extending across 1947–1949, not a single event on one day.
- The loss of homes and communities transformed Palestinian society across generations.
7. Q: How did the territorial division of Palestine after 1948 affect Palestinian political identity?
A: Show answer
After the war, the territory was divided among Israel, Jordan, and Egypt. The West Bank was annexed by Jordan in 1949, the Gaza Strip came under Egyptian military administration, and the remaining parts of Palestine became part of Israel. Palestinians were therefore governed under different legal and political systems, making it difficult for outsiders to identify a single center of Palestinian political life. This fragmentation weakened common institutions and limited communication, but it did not eliminate shared memories, symbols, or claims. The chapter presents territorial division as a major reason why Palestinian identity became less visible, not as proof that it ceased to exist.
Study instruction:
- Map the three principal post-1948 zones of Palestinian residence and compare the political status of their inhabitants.
Key point:
- Territorial fragmentation weakened common political organization.
- Shared identity could persist across different legal and administrative regimes.
8. Q: How did the political status of Palestinians differ in Jordan, Israel, Gaza, and the wider diaspora after 1948?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the largest single group, in Jordan and the annexed West Bank, received Jordanian nationality but remained in an uneasy political relationship with a state whose power structure they did not control. Fewer than 200,000 Palestinians remained inside Israel and obtained citizenship, but lived under military rule until 1966 and faced restrictions on Palestinian political expression. Palestinians in Gaza lived under Egyptian military administration, while refugees in Syria, Lebanon, and elsewhere experienced different categories of status and restrictions. These differences shaped how people could organize and express identity, even as a common Palestinian affiliation remained meaningful.
Study instruction:
- Compare citizenship, military administration, refugee status, and freedom of political organization across the post-1948 locations described.
Key point:
- Palestinians experienced different legal statuses after the territorial division.
- Political restrictions shaped public expression without erasing common identity.
9. Q: Why did refugee camps, workplaces, schools, and universities become important sites for the reconstitution of Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
These settings brought displaced Palestinians together after the destruction of their former communities. Refugee camps preserved shared experiences of loss and created new social networks. Workplaces and educational institutions connected people from different towns and classes, while universities provided settings for political discussion and organization. Khalidi describes these environments as the early social base from which a new generation of nationalist groups emerged in the 1950s. The camps were not simply places of passive dependency; they became important sites where a shared experience of dispossession could be interpreted politically and converted into collective organization.
Study instruction:
- Identify the social functions of camps, workplaces, and educational institutions in rebuilding networks after mass displacement.
Key point:
- Displacement fragmented older communities but also created new spaces for collective organization.
- Shared experiences in institutions helped sustain and renew Palestinian political identity.
10. Q: What was the significance of the Palestinian Students’ Union founded at Cairo University in 1950?
A: Show answer
The Union of Palestinian Students was one of the early post-1948 organizations through which a new generation began to organize independently. Khalidi identifies Yasser ‘Arafat, then a young engineering student, and Salah Khalaf, later known as Abu Iyyad, among those involved in its establishment. The organization connected students who had been displaced from Palestine and provided a setting for political discussion and collective action. It illustrates how universities outside Palestine became important centers of Palestinian national organization during the years when no single Palestinian territorial institution could perform that role.
Study instruction:
- Explain how student associations can preserve political identity and create leadership networks among a dispersed population.
Key point:
- Student organizations were early institutional foundations of renewed Palestinian political activity.
- The new leadership increasingly emerged from younger, educated generations.
11. Q: What other early Palestinian organizations emerged in Beirut and the Gaza Strip during the 1950s?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a student grouping at the American University of Beirut associated with George Habash and grassroots militant organizations in Gaza associated with Khalil al-Wazir, later known as Abu Jihad. These initiatives differed in structure and political approach, but they shared an orientation toward independent Palestinian action. Their emergence shows that Palestinian organization was not confined to one university or one country of refuge. Small, vulnerable groups were developing across several settings and gradually formed a network that could later become more visible in regional politics.
Study instruction:
- Compare the settings in which early groups emerged and identify how education, exile, and local conditions influenced their organization.
Key point:
- Palestinian political reorganization took place across several countries and territories.
- Small early groups created networks that later expanded into larger movements.
12. Q: How does Khalidi demonstrate continuity between the pre-1948 national movement and the clandestine groups of the 1950s and 1960s?
A: Show answer
He identifies continuity in the emphasis on historical rootedness in the land, Palestinian symbols, and opposition to Zionism. The new organizations also inherited the idea that Palestinians shared a distinct national cause requiring independent action. Their membership and leadership differed sharply from the older movement, but their political vocabulary and objectives drew on earlier decades of organizing. Khalidi argues that the post-1948 groups should therefore be understood as a new generation within a continuing national movement, not as evidence that a separate identity suddenly appeared in the 1960s.
Study instruction:
- Compare the themes, symbols, and political objectives of the Mandate-era movement with those of the post-1948 organizations.
Key point:
- Organizational discontinuity did not mean ideological or identity discontinuity.
- The post-1948 generation built on a national movement with earlier roots.
13. Q: Why did the older Palestinian political elite lose its position after 1948?
A: Show answer
The traditional elite was widely blamed for the loss of Palestine and was weakened by the destruction of its economic base. Families that had dominated politics during the Mandate lost land, businesses, and property, while many older leaders were displaced or discredited. The new organizations were led by younger men from poorer or middle-class backgrounds, educated in Mandate-era schools and often at universities in Cairo, Beirut, and Damascus. Khalidi presents this as a major social transformation in Palestinian politics: authority shifted from established notables to younger activists who had experienced defeat and exile and who were more willing to organize independently.
Study instruction:
- Compare the social background, education, and political legitimacy of the pre-1948 elite and the post-1948 activist generation.
Key point:
- The Nakba discredited and materially weakened the old political leadership.
- A younger generation built new organizations and leadership networks.
14. Q: How did pan-Arabism contribute to the apparent “disappearance” of a distinct Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
Pan-Arabism presented Arabs as one people with a shared language, history, and culture, divided by imperial arrangements rather than by genuine differences. This powerful ideology could subsume the identities of separate Arab countries, including Palestine, within a wider Arab community. Palestinians themselves were attracted to pan-Arabism because it promised external support against a much stronger Israeli adversary and offered refugees a larger sense of belonging. However, outside observers sometimes interpreted Arab identification as proof that Palestinians were not a distinct people. Khalidi argues that this was a false choice: Palestinians could be both Arabs and Palestinians, with the two affiliations serving different purposes.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a transnational ideology can provide solidarity while also obscuring the political identity of a particular national community.
Key point:
- Pan-Arabism could strengthen Palestinian mobilization and make Palestinian particularism less visible at the same time.
