- Arab identity and Palestinian identity were compatible rather than mutually exclusive.
15. Q: Why did Gamal ‘Abd al-Nasir’s pan-Arabism appeal strongly to Palestinians in the 1950s and 1960s?
A: Show answer
Palestinians had long identified with Arabism, and Nasser’s version appeared to offer a powerful regional movement capable of overcoming the weakness of dispersed Palestinian communities. It promised political and material support against Israel and gave refugees a wider identity that could protect them from marginalization in host countries. Nasser’s image and the radio station Sawt al-‘Arab were influential in Palestinian homes and camps. For many young activists, pan-Arabism was both an ideal of Arab unity and a practical means of mobilizing resources for the liberation of Palestine.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the ideological appeal of Arab unity from the practical expectation that a regional power could help Palestinians achieve their national objectives.
Key point:
- Pan-Arabism offered solidarity, scale, and political hope to a dispersed people.
- Its appeal was connected to the perceived need for outside support against Israel.
16. Q: In what sense was some Palestinian commitment to pan-Arabism instrumental?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that some Palestinians supported pan-Arab organizations because they expected Arab mobilization to help achieve the specific goal of returning to and liberating Palestine. Their participation in organizations with branches across the Arab world did not necessarily mean that their ultimate political objective was to dissolve Palestinian identity into a single Arab state. For example, the Movement of Arab Nationalists had an Arabist form and a broad regional presence, but its leadership and central objectives were heavily Palestinian. The distinction between the ideology an organization publicly used and the political purpose of its members is therefore important.
Study instruction:
- Analyze an organization’s public ideology separately from its leadership composition, recruitment base, and practical political objectives.
Key point:
- Pan-Arab language could be used as a means to pursue a specifically Palestinian goal.
- Organizational form does not always reveal the full identity or motivation of its members.
17. Q: How did the Movement of Arab Nationalists evolve into a Palestinian political formation after 1967?
A: Show answer
The Movement of Arab Nationalists had a pan-Arab structure and branches in several Arab countries, but its leadership and central objectives were strongly Palestinian. After the defeat of 1967 and the loss of confidence in Nasserism, it transformed rapidly into the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine. Khalidi uses this change to show how an ostensibly pan-Arab organization could become a specifically Palestinian movement when the regional political framework failed. The shift also illustrates how the same activists could move between broader Arabist and more particularist forms without abandoning their commitment to Palestine.
Study instruction:
- Trace the movement’s transition from regional Arabist organization to Palestinian formation and identify the political event that accelerated the change.
Key point:
- The 1967 defeat weakened pan-Arabism and accelerated Palestinian political particularism.
- The PFLP emerged from an organization whose practical center was already strongly Palestinian.
18. Q: Why did the 1967 war mark a turning point in Palestinian attitudes toward pan-Arabism?
A: Show answer
Israel’s defeat of the armies of Egypt, Syria, and Jordan undermined the claim that pan-Arab mobilization could deliver Palestine’s liberation. Israel also occupied the Gaza Strip and West Bank, the remaining parts of Palestine under Arab control after 1948, as well as the Sinai Peninsula and Golan Heights. Many Palestinians consequently turned toward organizations that emphasized independent Palestinian action, especially Fateh. The chapter argues that pan-Arabism did not disappear as a cultural ideal, but it lost much of its credibility as a practical political strategy. The defeat exposed the gap between Arab rhetoric and military capability.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a military defeat can change confidence in a political ideology without immediately eliminating its cultural appeal.
Key point:
- The 1967 defeat weakened pan-Arabism as a strategy for Palestinian liberation.
- Palestinian organizations advocating independent action gained support.
19. Q: Why did some Western observers treat Palestinian identity and Arab identity as mutually exclusive?
A: Show answer
Khalidi argues that Western observers often applied a model in which a person must belong primarily to one nation, as in an idealized version of French or American national identity. This made it difficult for them to understand that Palestinians could identify as Arabs while also possessing a distinct Palestinian national identity. The same misunderstanding applied to other layered identities in the Arab world. It contributed to the false conclusion that Palestinian Arabism disproved Palestinian nationhood. The chapter stresses that multiple affiliations are normal historical phenomena and should not be treated as evidence of artificiality or nonexistence.
Study instruction:
- Identify assumptions about exclusive national identity in a historical argument and test them against evidence of overlapping affiliations.
Key point:
- A person can belong to both a wider cultural nation and a distinct territorial community.
- Exclusive identity models can distort the interpretation of Arab and Palestinian history.
20. Q: What external political interests helped keep the myth of pan-Arabism alive in Western and Israeli discourse?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes several opponents of Nasserism, including British colonial figures, French leaders hostile to Arab support for North African independence movements, and American anti-Communist policymakers concerned about Egypt’s relationship with the Soviet Union. Israel and its supporters also viewed a united Arab coalition as a potential existential threat. These interests helped magnify the perceived power of pan-Arabism even after its practical political strength declined. The ideology became a symbolic adversary that could be used to mobilize opposition to Arab movements and to portray Palestinians as merely part of a vast, undifferentiated Arab world.
Study instruction:
- Map the different strategic concerns of British, French, American, and Israeli opponents of Nasserism and explain how they shaped public narratives.
Key point:
- Political opponents had incentives to exaggerate the reach and threat of pan-Arabism.
- The image of a unified Arab enemy could outlive the movement’s actual political strength.
21. Q: How did the 1956 Suez invasion affect the international perception of pan-Arabism?
A: Show answer
The tripartite Israeli-British-French invasion of Egypt in 1956 was part of the wider struggle over Nasser’s regional influence. Its failure weakened British and French power in the Middle East but did not end hostility toward Nasser among Western policymakers. Khalidi notes that Nasser continued to symbolize a threatening Arab force to some American and Israeli observers. The episode illustrates how pan-Arabism became entangled with anti-colonial politics, Cold War concerns, and regional security calculations. These conflicts helped sustain a highly charged image of Arab unity that often obscured differences among Arab societies and political movements.
Study instruction:
- Explain how the Suez crisis linked anti-colonial nationalism, Cold War strategy, and perceptions of Arab regional power.
Key point:
- The Suez invasion strengthened Nasser’s symbolic standing after its failure.
- External hostility helped preserve a simplified image of pan-Arabism as a unified threat.
22. Q: Why could the myth of pan-Arabism be used to weaken the legitimacy of the Palestinian claim to Palestine?
A: Show answer
If Palestinians were portrayed only as members of a much larger Arab nation, critics could argue that they had many other Arab countries in which to live and therefore no distinct claim to Palestine. This argument made Palestinian identity appear redundant and allowed the competing Israeli claim to be presented without a legitimate Palestinian counterpart. Khalidi argues that the logic was politically useful to those seeking to deny Palestinian national existence. The actual coexistence of Arab and Palestinian identities undermines the argument: a wider cultural affiliation does not erase a people’s historical connection to a particular territory.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the logic of the “they are only Arabs” argument and identify the false assumption that connects cultural identity to territorial entitlement.
Key point:
- Pan-Arab identity does not negate a distinct Palestinian national claim.
- The “many other Arab countries” argument depends on treating layered identities as mutually exclusive.
23. Q: How did Palestinian frustration with Ottoman policy before 1914 foreshadow later experiences under the British Mandate?
A: Show answer
Before the First World War, Palestinians repeatedly tried to persuade the Ottoman government to restrain Zionist immigration and land acquisition. Peasant resistance, parliamentary interventions, newspaper editorials, and local protests produced little change. Under the British Mandate, Palestinians again found that petitions, delegations, and public argument could not alter the fundamental policy supporting the Zionist national home. Khalidi sees a continuity in the experience of being unable to influence the governing power that determined Palestine’s future. The frustration was not simply a matter of disagreement with a government; it was a recurring failure to obtain meaningful political representation and control.
Study instruction:
- Compare Palestinian efforts to influence Ottoman and British authorities, noting the different institutions available and the similar limits of their effectiveness.
Key point:
- Political frustration with external rulers predated the Mandate and continued under British rule.
- Repeated failure to obtain representation deepened the sense of a shared national grievance.
24. Q: Why were land sales by Arab landlords a recurring source of frustration in Palestinian nationalist discourse?
A: Show answer
Palestinian writers argued that the growth of Zionist settlement depended partly on the willingness of absentee and local landlords to sell land. They criticized both the Ottoman government for failing to enforce restrictions and members of Palestinian society for pursuing private interests despite the wider political consequences. This critique appeared before 1914 and continued after the war. Khalidi emphasizes that the issue exposed a difficult internal problem: activists could protest foreign policy, but they also struggled to persuade their own society to act collectively. The persistence of land sales weakened Palestinian territorial security and reinforced frustration with the political elite.
Study instruction:
- Analyze the relationship between private property transactions, state regulation, collective political interests, and the limits of nationalist mobilization.
Key point:
- Land sales exposed tensions between individual economic decisions and collective national objectives.
- Palestinian activists faced challenges both from external governments and from internal social divisions.
25. Q: Why did Palestinians object to the absence of elected representative institutions under the British Mandate?
A: Show answer
Palestinians expected that representative government would allow the Arab majority to influence legislation and administration. British authorities prevented the creation of elected bodies because such institutions would likely have reflected the majority’s opposition to the Balfour Declaration and could have undermined the policy of supporting a Jewish national home. The chapter describes this as a major form of discrimination compared with other Arab territories where local governments, parliaments, or Arab rulers were established. The absence of representative institutions meant that petitions and delegations could express Palestinian demands but could not convert majority opinion into binding political decisions.
Study instruction:
- Explain how the denial of representative institutions can turn a majority population into a politically subordinate community.
Key point:
- British policy restricted the ability of Palestinians to exercise majority political preferences.
- The absence of elected representation intensified the national grievance.
26. Q: What did the 1921 al-Sabah editorial reveal about Palestinian comparisons with other Arab territories?
A: Show answer
The editorial argued that Palestine would have had fewer problems without the Balfour Declaration. It compared Palestine unfavorably with Iraq, Syria, and Jordan, where Arab governments, parliaments, or rulers existed in some form. The writer asked why Palestine should receive less political autonomy than neighboring territories. Khalidi uses the comparison to show that Palestinians understood their situation as exceptional because Zionist policy prevented the establishment of representative Arab institutions. The editorial also demonstrates that Palestinian nationalism was developing through comparison with the political arrangements of other emerging Arab states.
Study instruction:
- Compare the political arrangements described for Palestine, Iraq, Syria, and Jordan in the early 1920s and identify the source of Palestinian grievance.
Key point:
- Palestinians viewed the denial of representative government as a special injustice.
- Comparisons with neighboring Arab territories helped clarify a distinct Palestinian political claim.
27. Q: How did Palestinian national conferences and the 1921 delegation to London attempt to influence British policy?
A: Show answer
Palestinians held national conferences, sent telegrams and protests, met British officials such as Winston Churchill, and dispatched a delegation to London in 1921. The delegation sought to present Palestinian objections to British policy and demand a national government responsible to an elected parliamentary assembly. These activities show that Palestinian politics included organized constitutional and diplomatic efforts, not only street protest or armed resistance. However, the British government did not change the fundamental direction of its policy. Khalidi emphasizes the gap between the legitimacy of Palestinian demands for representation and the limited ability of the movement to secure them.
Study instruction:
- Identify the methods used by the national movement and distinguish the political demands from the limited results achieved.
Key point:
- Palestinian activists used conferences, diplomacy, petitions, and delegations to seek self-government.
- British refusal to alter policy deepened frustration and weakened confidence in constitutional channels.
28. Q: How did factional divisions within the Palestinian elite weaken the national movement during the Mandate?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a growing polarization between the faction led by Hajj Amin al-Husayni and another associated with Raghib al-Nashashibi. These divisions became more serious during the 1930s and were exploited by British authorities and Zionist organizations. Competition among leaders weakened collective decision-making and made it harder to sustain a unified strategy. The problem was compounded by the gap between established elites and younger activists, landless urban workers, indebted peasants, and other groups dissatisfied with the leadership. The chapter treats internal division as a major factor in the movement’s inability to respond effectively to mounting political and demographic pressure.
Study instruction:
- Map the main elite factions and identify how leadership rivalry affected representation, mobilization, and strategic coordination.
Key point:
- Elite factionalism weakened the national movement’s capacity for collective action.
- Internal divisions created opportunities for external powers to manipulate Palestinian politics.
29. Q: Why did younger Palestinians and poorer social groups become increasingly dissatisfied with the traditional leadership?
A: Show answer
Younger activists, landless urban poor, and indebted rural populations believed that the established elite was ineffective in defending Palestinian interests. Economic hardship in the early 1930s and rapid Jewish immigration increased the sense of urgency. The old leadership’s factional struggles and reliance on diplomacy appeared unable to prevent land loss or political subordination. This discontent created space for new forms of popular mobilization and for figures such as Shaykh ‘Izz al-Din al-Qassam, whose willingness to confront British authority resonated with people frustrated by elite politics. The chapter links social grievances to the emergence of more radical political action.
Study instruction:
- Explain how economic inequality, landlessness, debt, and political exclusion can produce challenges to an established national leadership.
Key point:
- Social and economic grievances undermined the legitimacy of the traditional elite.
- New activist currents emerged from groups that felt excluded from existing political leadership.
30. Q: Why was Shaykh ‘Izz al-Din al-Qassam’s death in 1935 politically significant?
A: Show answer
Al-Qassam’s armed revolt in the north was quickly suppressed by British troops, and he was killed in the confrontation. Khalidi argues that his death resonated widely because he appeared more closely aligned with popular Palestinian sentiment than the established elite. His martyrdom became an important symbol for later nationalist organizations and was interpreted as an example of resistance to British rule and Zionist settlement. The event helped demonstrate that popular dissatisfaction had moved beyond petitions and factional politics. It also became part of a later narrative that presented armed struggle as the authentic expression of national resistance.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the immediate military outcome of al-Qassam’s revolt from its later symbolic and political significance.
Key point:
- Al-Qassam’s death became a powerful symbol of popular resistance.
- Historical memory can give an unsuccessful revolt a lasting mobilizing role.
31. Q: What were the main features of the Palestinian general strike and revolt of 1936–1939?
A: Show answer
The revolt began with a spontaneous general strike in April 1936 and developed into a three-year uprising. It involved broad popular participation, a temporary convergence of different political forces, and later armed resistance. British forces initially lost control of parts of the country, including cities such as Jerusalem and Nablus. The revolt was eventually suppressed by a large-scale campaign involving tens of thousands of troops and aircraft in 1938–1939. Khalidi emphasizes both its popular character and its devastating consequences for Palestinian society, especially the destruction of the economy and the loss of experienced fighters and leaders.
Study instruction:
- Outline the sequence from al-Qassam’s death to the general strike, armed revolt, British repression, and political aftermath.
Key point:
- The revolt demonstrated substantial popular mobilization and temporarily united diverse groups.
- Its suppression weakened the social, economic, and military foundations of the national movement.
32. Q: Why did the 1939 White Paper fail to resolve the Palestinian national conflict?
A: Show answer
The White Paper promised limits on Jewish immigration and eventual Palestinian independence within ten years, but the chapter argues that these commitments were not implemented as expected. The revelation of the Holocaust and the need for refuge for Jews escaping Nazi persecution made the immigration limits politically and practically difficult to enforce. Meanwhile, the Palestinian national movement had been severely weakened by the revolt and the loss of its leadership. The White Paper therefore did not restore Palestinian political capacity or secure the self-determination that had been promised. It became another example of British commitments that failed to produce the intended outcome for Palestinians.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the White Paper’s stated commitments from the political conditions that prevented their effective implementation.
Key point:
- Formal promises did not guarantee implementation or Palestinian self-government.
- The weakened Palestinian movement entered the next crisis without a unified leadership.
33. Q: How did the revolt of 1936–1939 weaken Palestinian society before the 1947–1949 war?
A: Show answer
Khalidi identifies several consequences: the Arab economy was damaged by strikes, boycotts, and British reprisals; about 5,000 Palestinians were killed and 10,000 wounded; many experienced military cadres were killed or executed; and the traditional leadership was fragmented and exiled. The Arab Higher Committee, which had brought leaders together at the start of the strike, was again divided by tactical disagreements. By the outbreak of the Second World War, the Palestinians lacked even the appearance of unified leadership. The chapter argues that this weakened capacity was one of the major reasons the Palestinians were unable to respond effectively to the crisis of 1947–1949.
Study instruction:
- Trace how the revolt affected economic resources, military cadres, leadership cohesion, and organizational continuity.
Key point:
- The revolt’s defeat had long-term effects beyond the immediate loss of political concessions.
- The national movement entered the 1947–1949 crisis deeply weakened and divided.
34. Q: What structural disadvantages did Palestinians face in 1947–1948 compared with the Zionist movement?
A: Show answer
Khalidi contrasts a Palestinian population of roughly 1.4 million, largely rural and poorly organized militarily, with a smaller but politically unified Jewish society in Palestine. The Zionist movement possessed centralized institutions, a stronger economy, better organization, and a more developed military structure. Its settlements and land purchases had created territorial contiguity in a pattern the chapter describes as resembling an “N.” It also benefited from international support, including backing for partition by the United States and Soviet Union, and from understandings with Jordan’s ruler. The disparity in organization, resources, and external support contributed to the decisive outcome of the war.
Study instruction:
- Compare political unity, military organization, economic capacity, territorial presence, and international support on the two sides.
Key point:
- Military outcomes reflected major differences in organization and resources, not simply population size.
- Palestinian political fragmentation compounded the effects of the material disparity.
35. Q: Why does the chapter treat Palestinian defeat in 1948 as the culmination of earlier failures?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects the Nakba to a long sequence of difficulties: the inability to influence Ottoman policy before 1914, failure to secure representative government under the Mandate, internal divisions, ineffective leadership, social inequality, and the destruction of the 1936–1939 revolt. By 1947–1948, Palestinians lacked centralized military forces, reliable allies, and a unified leadership, while the Zionist movement possessed stronger institutions and external backing. Khalidi argues that the defeat was therefore not an isolated accident but the outcome of accumulated structural and political weaknesses. This interpretation does not deny the overwhelming power of external forces; it also examines Palestinian decisions and institutional failures.
Study instruction:
- Construct a causal chain linking pre-war political limitations, Mandate-era divisions, the revolt’s consequences, and the 1948 outcome.
Key point:
- The Nakba followed a cumulative weakening of Palestinian political and organizational capacity.
- Historical analysis must examine both external power and internal weaknesses.
36. Q: How did Arab states benefit politically or territorially from Palestinian defeat in 1948?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that Jordan absorbed the West Bank and East Jerusalem, becoming the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan; Egypt took control of the Gaza Strip; and Lebanon gained commercial advantages when Haifa lost its role as a major regional transit port and oil pipelines were rerouted through Lebanese territory. These outcomes gave some Arab governments or economies interests that did not always align with Palestinian independence. Khalidi argues that some Arab states had a vested interest in the post-war arrangements, although this could not be admitted openly because Arab public opinion was strongly sympathetic to Palestine.
Study instruction:
- Identify the territorial, political, and economic consequences of the 1948 settlement for Jordan, Egypt, and Lebanon.
Key point:
- Palestinian defeat created opportunities for neighboring states as well as Israel.
- Regional governments did not always share the same interests as Palestinian national aspirations.
37. Q: How did Arab public sympathy for Palestinians affect the policies of Arab governments after 1948?
A: Show answer
Public opinion in Cairo, Beirut, Damascus, and other Arab centers had long been sympathetic to Palestinians and suspicious of Zionism. This pressure encouraged newly independent Arab governments to support the Palestinians, sometimes even when their strategic interests suggested caution. At the same time, the Palestine issue became a political instrument in domestic and inter-Arab rivalries. Governments could accuse one another of failing Palestine while pursuing their own territorial or political interests. Once Palestinians reemerged as an independent actor in the mid-1960s, they could also use popular Arab sympathy to pressure governments and play them against one another.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish popular solidarity from state policy and identify how the Palestine issue could be used in regional political competition.
Key point:
- Arab public sympathy constrained governments but did not eliminate conflicting state interests.
- Palestinians later used that sympathy as a source of political leverage.
38. Q: What are the three stages in Khalidi’s account of the broadening of Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
The first stage, before the First World War, involved a relatively restricted urban and literate stratum for whom Palestinian identification competed with Ottomanism, Arabism, religion, and local loyalties. The second stage followed the First World War, when political shocks and the Mandate expanded Palestinian identity to much wider parts of the population and made shared fate more central. The third stage followed 1948, when displacement, refugee life, and the shared trauma of defeat reduced earlier social differences and helped universalize Palestinian identity. The stages describe a broad historical pattern rather than an identical experience for every individual.
Study instruction:
- Summarize each stage by its principal social base, historical catalyst, and relationship to other forms of identity.
Key point:
- Palestinian identity broadened from an elite focus to a wider shared identity over time.
- The Nakba was a decisive stage in its universalization.
39. Q: What characterized the first stage of Palestinian identity formation before the First World War?
A: Show answer
The first stage was concentrated among urban, literate, and educated people, including teachers, clerks, officials, and businesspeople who formed a new middle stratum beyond the older notables. Palestinian identity was meaningful but not exclusive. It coexisted with Ottomanism, Arabism, religious affiliations, and older local and family loyalties. The chapter emphasizes that the rural and illiterate majority did not necessarily share the same level of political consciousness or vocabulary. This early stage created important foundations, but the sense of Palestinian identity had not yet become a primary or universal category for the population.
Study instruction:
- Identify the social groups most visible in pre-war sources and explain why their political language cannot automatically be generalized to the entire population.
Key point:
- Early Palestinian identity was strongest among urban and educated groups.
- It overlapped with other loyalties and had not yet become universal.
40. Q: How did the post-First World War period expand Palestinian identity beyond its earlier elite base?
A: Show answer
The shocks of war, the collapse of Ottomanism, British occupation, the Balfour Declaration, and the failure of the Syrian and wider Arab projects gave Palestinians a stronger sense of shared fate. Political organization and the press increasingly focused on Palestine’s specific problems. Education also expanded, helping transmit common geographical and national concepts beyond the initial urban elite. The Mandate’s repeated conflicts with British authorities and Zionist institutions further strengthened a sense that Palestinians faced common external challenges. The chapter describes this as a second stage in which Palestinian identity became a primary category for many more people, though social differences remained.
Study instruction:
- Link the major post-war political shocks to changes in political organization, press language, and educational exposure.
Key point:
- The post-war period widened the social reach of Palestinian identity.
- Shared political threats and institutions helped make it a primary focus for many.
41. Q: How did the events of 1948 help universalize Palestinian identity across social classes?
A: Show answer
The Nakba affected urban and rural Palestinians, wealthy and poor, educated and less educated. Many people were displaced into refugee camps, where children from different backgrounds shared similar conditions and increasingly received education through UNRWA. The common experience of losing homes, land, and political control reduced the significance of some pre-war divisions. Even Palestinians who remained in their homes experienced the trauma and political consequences of 1948. Khalidi argues that this shared experience helped make Palestinian identity more uniform and widely recognized within the population than it had been before the catastrophe.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a common historical experience can reduce social distinctions and strengthen a shared group identity.
Key point:
- The Nakba was a common experience that brought different Palestinian social groups closer together.
- Displacement and refugee institutions helped transmit a shared identity to younger generations.
42. Q: What role did UNRWA education play in the post-1948 consolidation of Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that children in refugee camps received education through UNRWA, something many had not been able to obtain under the British Mandate. Within a generation, literacy among the young became nearly universal in the camps. This educational experience helped connect children from different towns, villages, and social backgrounds through common institutions and shared memories of displacement. UNRWA did not create Palestinian identity by itself, but it provided an important institutional environment in which a common identity could be transmitted and reinforced after the destruction of many pre-1948 community structures.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish the humanitarian function of refugee education from its wider social effects on literacy, collective memory, and identity.
Key point:
- Education in refugee camps helped sustain and transmit Palestinian identity across generations.
- Shared institutions can rebuild social cohesion after displacement.
43. Q: Why does Khalidi describe Palestinian identity as having been “universalized” by the refugee experience?
A: Show answer
Before 1948, Palestinian identity was one among several overlapping affiliations and was strongest in particular social strata. After the Nakba, Palestinians across different territories and classes shared dispossession, exile, and dependence on new authorities. Refugee status and the loss of control over their own lives became common experiences that crossed many earlier social divisions. The chapter argues that if some people had been uncertain about their identity before 1948, the defeat and displacement made it unmistakable afterwards. “Universalized” refers to the broad reach of the identity among Palestinians, not to the disappearance of all political or cultural differences.
Study instruction:
- Explain how shared loss and common treatment by host authorities can turn a previously uneven identity into a widespread collective affiliation.
Key point:
- Shared dispossession helped make Palestinian identity widely recognized across the population.
- Universalization did not require complete uniformity of political views or social experience.
44. Q: How could Palestinian identity survive without the machinery of an independent nation-state?
A: Show answer
Unlike state-based national identities, Palestinian identity developed and persisted without a sovereign government controlling the whole population and territory. Khalidi compares Palestinians with other national groups that maintained identity without a state, including Kurds, Armenians, and Jews in Palestine before 1948. Palestinian institutions used newspapers, publishing houses, research centers, radio, student organizations, and the PLO to circulate symbols and narratives. These institutions could not match the reach of a state educational system or official media, but they helped maintain group solidarity and political memory across a dispersed population.
Study instruction:
- List non-state institutions that can transmit national identity and compare their reach with state-controlled schools, museums, and media.
Key point:
- Statehood is not a prerequisite for the survival of national identity.
- Non-state organizations and communications networks can maintain collective memory and solidarity.
45. Q: What does the narrative of “failure as triumph” mean in Palestinian political memory?
A: Show answer
Khalidi describes a narrative in which defeats and setbacks are reinterpreted as evidence of courage, sacrifice, and steadfastness in the face of overwhelming odds. This approach helps explain how a people can preserve dignity and solidarity despite repeated political and military failures. The chapter notes that the narrative can also conceal poor decisions, weak leadership, and missed opportunities by focusing almost entirely on the strength of the enemy. It therefore has both a mobilizing function and a potential cost: it sustains identity, but may discourage critical assessment of the movement’s own choices and responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a historical account of defeat and identify which elements emphasize heroism, which acknowledge strategic failure, and which are omitted.
Key point:
- Narratives of heroic endurance can preserve solidarity after defeat.
- They can also obscure internal responsibility and prevent learning from mistakes.
46. Q: How is the concept of sumud related to the Palestinian narrative of failure and survival?
A: Show answer
Sumud is commonly translated as steadfastness, but Khalidi explains that it carries a wider sense of enduring and surviving despite repeated setbacks. It became a central way of describing Palestinian persistence through wars, displacement, occupation, and political failure. The concept allows defeat to be understood not only as loss but also as a test of collective endurance. The chapter notes that this language was widely used in accounts of the fighting in Lebanon and resistance in the occupied territories. Sumud therefore links everyday persistence to a broader national story of continuity.
Study instruction:
- Explain how steadfastness can function as both a personal practice and a collective national symbol.
Key point:
- Sumud frames survival and continued attachment as forms of resistance.
- It helps connect individual endurance with national identity.
47. Q: How was Shaykh al-Qassam’s 1935 revolt incorporated into later Palestinian nationalist narratives?
A: Show answer
Later nationalist writers presented al-Qassam as a pioneer who challenged elite politics and showed the way toward popular armed struggle. His death was treated as martyrdom and as a catalyst for the 1936 revolt. Khalidi notes that accounts by writers such as Ghassan Kanafani emphasized his role in mobilizing the population and criticizing compromise with the British. These narratives helped provide the new movement with historical precedents for armed resistance. However, they sometimes presented the event as more decisive and coherent than the immediate historical record could support, illustrating how later movements reinterpret earlier struggles to serve contemporary political needs.
Study instruction:
- Compare the historical event with later commemorative accounts and identify how the meaning of al-Qassam’s actions changed over time.
Key point:
- Al-Qassam became a symbol linking Mandate-era resistance to later movements.
- Political memory often reinterprets earlier events to legitimize new strategies.
48. Q: How did later nationalist accounts portray the 1936–1939 revolt, and what did they often leave out?
A: Show answer
Later accounts emphasized the revolt’s popular character, the spontaneous general strike, armed resistance, and the betrayal of the movement by Arab governments and traditional Palestinian leaders. These themes highlighted courage and unity. Khalidi argues that such accounts often downplayed the scale of Palestinian losses, the limited chances of success, the movement’s internal divisions, and the strategic errors that weakened it. The retelling was shaped by the political needs of the 1960s, when activists were encouraging Palestinians to take their fate into their own hands and adopt armed struggle. Historical interpretation was therefore connected to contemporary mobilization.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a later narrative of the revolt by comparing its claims with the chapter’s account of casualties, repression, leadership divisions, and strategic limits.
Key point:
- The revolt became a source of inspiration for later nationalist movements.
- Heroic retellings sometimes omitted the costs and limitations of the uprising.
49. Q: How did the battle of al-Karama in March 1968 become a foundation myth for the Palestinian commando movement?
A: Show answer
Israeli forces attacked Palestinian bases near al-Karama in Jordan, expecting the commandos to withdraw. Instead, Fateh’s leadership decided to stand and fight, and Jordanian artillery also became involved. Israeli forces suffered heavier casualties than expected and left behind damaged vehicles, which were displayed in Amman. The battle was not a Palestinian military victory in a conventional sense: Palestinian losses were heavier and Israeli forces withdrew without losing prisoners. Its political importance came from the contrast with the Arab armies’ defeat in 1967. The episode was rapidly turned into a symbol of dignity, resistance, and renewed Palestinian agency.
Study instruction:
- Separate the military outcome from the political and symbolic consequences of al-Karama.
Key point:
- Al-Karama became a powerful symbol because Palestinians were seen to have stood and fought.
- The political impact of a battle can differ sharply from its military result.
50. Q: Why does Khalidi describe al-Karama as a political success but not a clear military victory?
A: Show answer
The Israeli forces inflicted heavier casualties on the Palestinian commandos than they suffered themselves and withdrew on the same day without losing prisoners or bodies. In strictly military terms, the battle did not reverse the balance of power. Yet the commandos had resisted rather than fled, and Jordanian artillery had inflicted losses on Israeli vehicles. After the 1967 defeat, this visible resistance had enormous symbolic value in the Arab world. Khalidi argues that the movement converted a limited battlefield episode into a political foundation myth that helped recruit supporters and increase the PLO’s influence.
Study instruction:
- Use separate criteria for military success, political legitimacy, morale, recruitment, and symbolic impact when assessing a battle.
Key point:
- Military defeat or stalemate can still produce major political effects.
- Al-Karama’s symbolism helped expand the Palestinian commando movement.
51. Q: What happened to the PLO in Jordan in September 1970, and how was the defeat later narrated?
A: Show answer
The PLO lost its position in Jordan after tensions escalated, including aircraft hijackings by a constituent group, the PFLP, which provoked the Hashemite regime. The Jordanian army then routed Palestinian forces in what became known as Black September and expelled the movement. Later narratives emphasized the overwhelming military power of the Jordanian state but often minimized the Palestinian movement’s political errors and its failure to maintain local support. Khalidi argues that this was another example of defeat being portrayed mainly as heroic resistance, rather than as an event requiring a full examination of strategic decisions and their consequences.
Study instruction:
- Identify both external military pressures and internal political decisions in the events leading to Black September.
Key point:
- Black September was a major organizational and political defeat for the PLO.
- Explanations that focus only on superior enemy power can conceal preventable mistakes.
52. Q: How did the Palestinian experience in Lebanon from 1975 to 1982 affect the PLO and the wider national movement?
A: Show answer
The PLO became involved in a complex Lebanese conflict that brought it into confrontation with local militias, Syria, and Israel. Palestinian refugee camps were overrun, inhabitants massacred or expelled, and the movement suffered severe losses during Israel’s 1982 invasion and siege of Beirut. Khalidi cites estimates of approximately 19,000 killed and 30,000 wounded during the 1982 fighting. The PLO leadership and institutions were then evacuated from Beirut and dispersed to distant locations, including Tunisia and several Arab states. The experience recalled the 1948 exodus and intensified debate about the movement’s strategic errors and its relationship with local populations.
Study instruction:
- Trace the sequence from the Lebanese conflict to the 1982 invasion, siege, evacuation, and dispersal of PLO institutions.
Key point:
- The Lebanon experience was a major military and political setback for the PLO.
- Loss of local support and strategic misjudgments became central issues in later criticism.
53. Q: What did ‘Isam Sirtawi’s comment at the 1982 Palestine National Council meeting reveal about Palestinian criticism of the PLO?
A: Show answer
Sirtawi sarcastically warned that if the evacuation of Beirut was described as a victory, the next PNC meeting might have to take place in an even more distant exile. His remark challenged the practice of presenting every defeat as a triumph. Shafiq al-Hout also criticized the leadership for describing the Lebanon disaster as a victory and accused it of misleading Palestinians. Khalidi notes the strong audience response to al-Hout’s speech, suggesting that many Palestinians were ready to question the leadership’s narrative. The episode shows the emergence of a counter-narrative emphasizing accountability and honest assessment.
Study instruction:
- Explain why internal criticism can become more visible after repeated setbacks and why the audience response matters as evidence of changing attitudes.
Key point:
- By the early 1980s, more Palestinians questioned the portrayal of defeats as victories.
- Accountability became a growing issue within the national movement.
54. Q: How did PLO media help maintain Palestinian identity across a dispersed population?
A: Show answer
The PLO lacked the institutions of a sovereign state, but it developed newspapers, periodicals, publishing houses, research centers, and radio broadcasting. Sawt Filastin, the Voice of Palestine, became an important source of news and political interpretation for Palestinians and in some settings replaced the influence of Egypt’s Sawt al-‘Arab. These media circulated common symbols and accounts of the national experience, helping maintain solidarity across different countries of residence. Khalidi notes that this narrative was often self-serving and emphasized the heroism of the movement while minimizing its errors, but it nevertheless provided a shared political framework for a dispersed people.
Study instruction:
- Identify the functions of print, research, and broadcast media in creating a shared public sphere when a population lacks a state.
Key point:
- Non-state media helped sustain a common Palestinian political narrative across exile.
- The same media could build solidarity while also promoting selective interpretations of history.
55. Q: What was the “inside versus outside” shift in Palestinian politics associated with the First Intifada?
A: Show answer
For many years, the center of Palestinian politics was located outside Palestine, in the diaspora and in the PLO’s external institutions. The spontaneous uprising in the occupied territories in December 1987 reestablished Palestine itself as the center of political activity. It took the PLO leadership by surprise and demonstrated the capacity of Palestinians living under occupation to mobilize independently. Khalidi argues that the intifada revitalized a national movement that had been weakened by exile and internal criticism. It also impressed international opinion and helped create conditions for the Madrid negotiations that began in 1991.
Study instruction:
- Compare the political role of diaspora institutions with the grassroots mobilization inside the occupied territories before and after December 1987.
Key point:
- The First Intifada shifted the center of political initiative back inside Palestine.
- Grassroots mobilization strengthened the national movement and changed international perceptions.
56. Q: What was Abu Jihad’s “Jordanian Option,” and why did it matter to the PLO’s strategy?
A: Show answer
Khalil al-Wazir argued that the PLO could not have a future unless it reestablished a presence inside the occupied territories. He believed this could be achieved through improved relations with Jordan, which would allow the movement to renew links with the West Bank and Gaza. The PLO leadership eventually pursued rapprochement with Jordan despite internal opposition. Khalidi describes the strategy as an acknowledgment that the movement’s future lay inside Palestine rather than in distant exile. However, he also argues that the initiative came too late to prevent the PLO’s declining status in regional politics.
Study instruction:
- Explain the strategic logic of reconnecting the PLO with the West Bank and Gaza through Jordan and identify the limits of the timing.
Key point:
- The Jordanian Option aimed to restore the movement’s presence inside Palestine.
- The shift reflected recognition that external exile could not remain the movement’s permanent center.
57. Q: What did the Madrid Conference of 1991 and the Oslo agreements of 1993 signify for Palestinian identity?
A: Show answer
The Madrid Conference brought a Palestinian delegation, as part of a joint delegation with Jordan, into direct negotiations with Israel for the first time. The 1993 Declaration of Principles between Israel and the PLO further recognized the PLO as a legitimate representative of the Palestinian people. Khalidi treats these developments as symbolically important because they marked a shift from mutual denial toward mutual recognition. They did not resolve the conflict or establish Palestinian sovereignty, but they confirmed that Palestinian identity had become an accepted political reality in international diplomacy and could no longer be dismissed as nonexistent.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish symbolic recognition, diplomatic participation, limited self-administration, and full sovereign statehood.
Key point:
- Madrid and Oslo made Palestinian national representation more visible and formally recognized.
- Recognition did not automatically deliver self-determination or independence.
58. Q: Why did Khalidi describe the Palestinian Authority created under the Oslo process as limited rather than a fully sovereign government?
A: Show answer
The interim arrangements gave the Palestinian Authority control over much of the Gaza population and many West Bank towns and villages, but not over Israeli settlements, military bases, strategic roads, borders, or much of the countryside. The chapter emphasizes that Israel retained ultimate security control and that the Authority’s jurisdiction was sharply circumscribed. Khalidi warned that interim arrangements could become a permanent framework for fragmented Palestinian enclaves rather than a contiguous sovereign state. The distinction between administrative responsibility and real sovereignty is therefore central to understanding the limits of the agreements as described in the chapter’s 1990s context.
Study instruction:
- Map the areas and functions under Palestinian Authority administration and distinguish them from the powers retained by Israel in the period described.
Key point:
- The Palestinian Authority exercised limited self-administration, not full sovereignty.
- Territorial fragmentation and retained Israeli control constrained the meaning of the interim arrangements.
59. Q: How did the Oslo process begin a “recentering” of Palestinian society inside Palestine?
A: Show answer
Exiles returned, PLO institutions were integrated with local structures, and Palestinian capital began to invest in the West Bank and Gaza. These developments shifted the practical center of Palestinian political and social life from the diaspora toward the homeland. Khalidi notes that the new Authority had more power over more Palestinians in more of Palestine than any Palestinian agency had possessed during the twentieth century, even though it was not sovereign. This created new responsibilities and accountability for Palestinian leaders. The chapter suggests that the movement could no longer attribute every failure to an external authority because it now exercised limited administrative power of its own.
Study instruction:
- Identify the institutional, demographic, and economic changes associated with the return of exiles and integration of PLO structures into local administration.
Key point:
- The Oslo process shifted political life toward institutions based inside Palestine.
- Limited governing authority created new obligations of accountability.
60. Q: Why does the chapter conclude that Palestinian identity represents both failure and success?
A: Show answer
It is a failure because Palestinians had not achieved self-determination, independence, or control over their country despite generations of struggle and sacrifice. In some respects, their political and territorial position was worse than in the late Ottoman period, when they had majority status across most of the country and participated in elections to the Ottoman Parliament. Yet it is also a success because Palestinian identity survived displacement, fragmentation, hostile policies, and repeated predictions of its disappearance. The chapter treats the continued attachment to Palestinian identity as a significant achievement, while leaving open whether that identity can become the basis for genuine self-determination and lasting political institutions.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter’s dual assessment by separating identity survival from the still-unresolved goals of sovereignty, independence, and territorial self-determination.
Key point:
- Palestinian identity survived despite repeated political and military defeats.
- Survival of identity is a significant achievement but does not substitute for self-determination or statehood.
Rows: 60
